Slice E: the live-msdb failed-jobs alert query â€” previously duplicated verbatim in both apps â€” now lives once in the shared library as `FailedJobsQuery` (the SQL text + the `DbDataReader` row mapping, clause-pinned by `FailedJobsQueryTests` in both suites; hosts keep their own connections and permission gating), ready for the Darling engine. Slice B: the LAST two builders â€” blocking and deadlock â€” are now shared too (`BuildBlockingContext`/`BuildDeadlockContext` with `IsDeadlockExcluded` and the deadlock-graph process-summary parse, Lite's grouped rendering canonical per the Phase-5 review; identity-pinned by `BlockingDeadlockContextBuilderTests`), and the collected-store read surface moved behind the new `IAlertReadAdapter` seam â€” all seven collected feeds (blocking with the XEâ†’DMV fallback CONTRACT, deadlocks, poison waits, long-running queries, volume free space, tempdb, anomalous jobs; failed jobs deliberately stay OUT, being a live-msdb read via `FailedJobsQuery`, not a collected feed) â€” so the slice-D engine will be store-free. Lite's `LiteAlertReadAdapter` wraps its existing DuckDB queries verbatim (the alert loop's only change: inline `_dataService` fetches became adapter calls â€” decisions, gating, and state untouched; Lite's row types now derive from the shared `BlockedProcessAlertRow`/`DeadlockAlertRow` so no mapping copy can drift), and the new `DarlingAlertReadAdapter` ports all seven reads to Postgres: raw collector tables (no `v_` views exist there), a parameterized naive-UTC now instead of DuckDB's bare `NOW()` (timestamptz vs naive-timestamp mismatch), no N'' literals, and the blocking read reproducing Lite's BPR-preferred + DMV-appended merge via the shared `BlockedProcessReportMerge` â€” pinned ungated (dialect/table/window assertions) and end-to-end against a dev Postgres (gated on DARLING_TEST_PG), including the DMV fallback row appearing exactly when no BPR overlaps. The Dashboard's async blocking/deadlock builders are deliberately UNCHANGED this slice â€” its rendering diverged, and convergence is a separately-planned migration. Slice D â€” the payoff: the shared **`AlertEngine`** itself, Lite's `CheckPerformanceAlerts` transplanted line-cited into the library â€” the same alert ORDER (CPU â†’ blocking â†’ deadlocks â†’ poison waits â†’ long-running queries â†’ tempdb â†’ volume free space â†’ anomalous jobs â†’ failed jobs), the same per-alert gating order (enabled â†’ fetch â†’ threshold â†’ edge-trigger â†’ mute â†’ cooldown â†’ deliver â†’ record state), the [#1091] `RollingCountAlertGate` MOVED into the library verbatim (Lite call sites alias it), the [#1145] restart-surviving watermarks seeded per-server through `IAlertStateStore`, the [#754] low-disk worsening gate + [#1136] CRITICAL grading, the [#1141]-aware delivery through `IAlertDeliverer` (one `AlertOutcome` per fire; muted alerts delivered flagged-muted so hosts record without sending), CPU-mode selection INSIDE the engine (`AlertServerSnapshot` carries both CPU values; the engine applies Lite's `CpuPercentForAlert` rule), suppression as an INPUT (evaluate-but-don't-deliver; gates don't advance where Lite's don't), failed jobs via a host-supplied live-msdb fetcher (gated online + !AzureSqlDb; NO msdb probe per the review â€” hosts degrade denied reads to empty), and Lite's tray-only "Resolved/Cleared" toasts surfaced as an optional resolution callback carrying Lite's exact strings (tray/badges/delivery-mode split stay app-side). Engine evaluations are per-server serialized (concurrent across servers), and `AlertEngineTests` pins every gating semantic against fakes with the source Lite line cited per pin. The **Darling headless service is the engine's first consumer â€” the service now alerts**: a V3 "alerting-stores" migration adds Postgres twins of Lite's three alerting tables (`config_alert_log`, `config_edge_trigger_watermarks` with its (server_id, metric_name) upsert key, `config_mute_rules`), served by `PgAlertStateStore`/`PgAlertHistoryStore`/`PgMuteRuleStore` (Lite's DuckDB stores ported statement-for-statement, `INSERT OR REPLACE` â†’ `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, including the [#1154] per-fingerprint cooldown-seed LIKE); darling.json grows optional `alerts` (every default mirrors Lite's App defaults exactly) + `smtp`/`webhooks` sections (DPAPI --encrypt-password blob for the SMTP password; SMTP enabled by host+from+to, a webhook by its URL â€” no speculative flags) behind `DarlingAlertSettings`, which implements BOTH the engine-threshold and delivery-settings seams; and `DarlingAlertDeliverer` reproduces Lite's record cadence â€” the shared `EmailSendCore` + `WebhookAlertService` fan-out, then ONE combined `config_alert_log` row per fire even when no channel is configured. The worker evaluates each connected server after its collector sweep on Lite's 30-second overview cadence, with a live-msdb failed-jobs fetcher degrading permission errors (229/297/300/916) to an empty list exactly like the Dashboard's caller. A gated end-to-end test (DARLING_TEST_PG) plants a deadlock + poison wait, fires the engine over the REAL PG stores, asserts the [#1140] fingerprint, the history row, the persisted watermark â€” and that neither the same engine (cooldown + watermark) nor a fresh engine (post-restart watermark seed) re-fires the same deadlock. Lite's own loop is NOT rewired this slice â€” that forwarding change ships separately. The forwarding slice then rewires **Lite's own alert loop onto the shared engine** â€” the same evaluation code Darling runs â€” with zero behavior change, pinned: `CheckPerformanceAlerts` collapses to snapshot-build â†’ `AlertEngine.EvaluateServerAsync` â†’ badge writes, and Lite's five seam implementations carry everything app-specific â€” `AppAlertEngineSettings` (live App.* pass-through incl. the six long-running-query read knobs), the existing `LiteAlertReadAdapter`, `LiteAlertStateStore` (the [#1145] watermarks over the same DuckDB `config_edge_trigger_watermarks` rows, every call `Task.Run`-wrapped off the dispatcher per the [#1202] rule; the old bulk startup seed is superseded by the engine's per-key seed-before-first-sweep, so pre-upgrade watermarks still prevent post-restart re-fires), and `LiteAlertDeliverer` (tray toast first unless muted â€” titles/bodies/severities verbatim via the engine's new per-fire `ShortMessage`, Error icon for deadlocks/poison waits â€” then the [#1141]/[#1236] per-event-vs-summary send split for blocking/deadlocks only and the direct one-combined-history-row send for everything else, exactly the old routing). The tray-only "Resolved/Cleared" toasts ride the engine's resolution callback with the old exact strings, and the [#754]/[#749] server-tab badges are fed by the engine's new `AlertSweepResult` (the two standing conditions, observed before the suppression/watermark gates, so acknowledged/silenced servers still light badges and disabled checks still clear them â€” [#1128] semantics intact). `LiteAlertForwardingTests` pins the equivalence scenario-by-scenario against the deleted loop with the old line cited per pin: CPU fire/resolve strings, blocking edge-trigger + watermark persist + restart seed, suppressed sweeps delivering nothing without advancing watermarks, muted alerts still recording history, low-disk worsening + CRITICAL grading, failed-job watermark dedup across restarts, every metric's toast body, and the deliverer's routing/split/override table
- **Darling: optional TimescaleDB adoption â€” hypertables, chunk-based retention, and compression as the archival tier** ([#1262]) â€” the storage-evolution slice. The service now detects TimescaleDB at runtime (`TimescaleSupport` in `PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Storage`): right after migration it attempts `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS timescaledb` (IF NOT EXISTS short-circuits before privilege checks, so an admin-pre-created extension works for a service account that could never create one) and confirms against `pg_extension` â€” **plain PostgreSQL remains fully supported**: a server without the loadable library degrades gracefully to plain-PG mode (logged once at Information, a supported configuration rather than a problem), and this is deliberately RUNTIME setup, not a versioned migration, so `PgMigrations` stays engine-plain and the same store works with or without the extension. When present, every collector table converts to a hypertable â€” `create_hypertable(..., by_range('<its own prefix time column>'), if_not_exists => true, migrate_data => true)` (validated live on TimescaleDB 2.28.1; existing plain-PG data migrates into chunks; the naive-UTC `timestamp` partition columns draw an expected, accepted advisory TIMESTAMPTZ warning â€” naive-UTC is the deliberate cross-store contract), per-table failure-isolated and re-converged on every start. Scope is EXACTLY the shared collector catalog, pinned by test: the collector tables were designed keyless for this conversion, while the registry/config tables (servers, collection_log, `config_*`, `analysis_muted`) keep their PRIMARY KEYs and stay plain (`analysis_findings` â€” also designed keyless â€” COULD convert later; deliberately not yet). The daily retention purge grows the extension-present branch `DarlingRetention`'s docs always promised: collector tables purge via `drop_chunks(..., older_than => make_interval(days => <shared RetentionDays>))` â€” a metadata-only detach of whole expired chunks instead of row-scanning DELETEs, with an accepted coarseness (rows in a partially-expired chunk survive until the whole chunk ages out, up to ~7 days of grace at the default chunk width) and a per-table DELETE fallback so a table that failed conversion still honors its horizon â€” while collection_log (and the analysis tables' own cleanup) stay DELETE-based; plain-PG stores keep the existing DELETE path untouched. And each collector table gets compression: `ALTER TABLE ... SET (timescaledb.compress, timescaledb.compress_segmentby = 'server_id')` plus a hardcoded 7-day `add_compression_policy` (defaults over speculative config; the long-stable pre-2.18 compression vocabulary on purpose â€” 2.18+ keeps it as a supported alias of the columnstore rebrand) â€” compressed chunks remain fully queryable, so **this IS Darling's archival tier**, the centralized-store answer to Lite's parquet archive. Pinned ungated (`TimescaleSupportTests`: hypertable scope vs the catalog with the registry exclusions, by_range on each table's own time column incl. the config snapshots' capture_time, if_not_exists/migrate_data everywhere, segmentby server_id, the 7-day if_not_exists policy shape; `DarlingRetentionTests`: drop_chunks carrying each table's own shared horizon) and proven end-to-end against a dev Postgres gated on DARLING_TEST_PG: detect â†’ convert all 26 (idempotent second pass) â†’ a 40-day-old wait_stats row vanishes with its whole chunk under the drop_chunks purge while a fresh row and the collection_log DELETE path hold â†’ the compression policy applies idempotently and its background job lands in `timescaledb_information.jobs`. Slice AN4 puts the analysis surface on the wire: **the service now exposes the six analysis MCP tools** â€” analyze_server, get_analysis_facts, compare_analysis, audit_config, get_analysis_findings, mute_analysis_finding â€” the SAME tool surface Lite and the Dashboard expose, with each tool body mirroring Lite's field-for-field (same response envelopes, the #1224 miss-vocabulary Status envelope, the shared FactAdvice/CoFiredSummary composition and the per-app ToolRecommendations copy) so MCP clients see one consistent product across all three SKUs; hosting mirrors Lite's model exactly (ModelContextProtocol.AspNetCore 1.4.0, Streamable HTTP on localhost, stateless mode + Gemini-compatible schemas for #1074, the port-in-use pre-check) as an optional hosted service gated by a new darling.json `mcp` section â€” **default OFF** (`{ "mcp": { "enabled": false, "port": 5152 } }`; a headless service opens no local port unprompted, and 5152 completes the family's non-colliding port map: Dashboard 5150, Lite 5151); server names resolve through the Postgres `servers` registry the worker upserts on connect (Lite's ServerResolver semantics â€” sole-server auto-select, exact-beats-partial on storage or display name, the available-servers error listing with the `[Read-Only]` tag derived from the `:RO` storage-name suffix â€” with the registry's shared-hash server_id passed through, never re-derived), and analyze_server's live plan fetches resolve connection strings from darling.json lazily per fetch. Pinned ungated (the six-tool surface via the MCP attributes â€” all static, the Gemini-registration requirement; the config default-off + family port; the full resolver semantics over fake registry rows) and end-to-end against a live Postgres gated on DARLING_TEST_PG: register servers the way the worker does, plant a persisted finding, call the tool methods directly â€” the finding round-trips through Lite's envelope field-for-field, the empty server returns the shared "empty" Status envelope, partial/display/unknown names resolve per the registry semantics, mute_analysis_finding writes the mute row and returns the muted envelope, and the mute then filters the same story from a subsequent analysis-run save
- **Darling: managed bundled Postgres â€” the zero-admin first-run bootstrap** ([#1262]) â€” the shipped-default story: install the service with NO existing PostgreSQL, and `{ "postgres": { "managed": true, "port": 5641, "dataDirectory": null } }` (now the sample's default) makes the first run stand the store up itself via the new `DarlingManagedPostgres`, BEFORE anything touches the store: locate the runtime shipped beside the service (`pg-runtime\pgsql\`, self-healing by extracting `pg-runtime.zip` when only the zip is present), `initdb` a fresh cluster with **scram-sha-256 and a generated 32-char crypto-random password â€” never trust auth, even on localhost** (trust would hand superuser to any local code that can open a loopback socket and fails every enterprise scan; the password is DPAPI-LocalMachine-protected into `pg-credential.dpapi` beside the data directory, the same machine-bound posture as `--encrypt-password`), append `shared_preload_libraries = 'timescaledb'` + the port + `listen_addresses = '127.0.0.1'` (loopback only; the append is marker-guarded and re-checked every start so a crash mid-bootstrap self-heals instead of silently degrading to plain PG), `pg_ctl start` windowed with the server-log tail surfaced on failure (stale postmaster.pid from a crash is pg_ctl's own recovery), and create the `darling` database â€” then the existing `TimescaleSupport` detects/converts/compresses as usual. The connection string is DERIVED (localhost + port + the stored credential), so setting `connectionString` too is a validation error; port 5641 is deliberately uncommon so the bundle coexists with any existing PostgreSQL, and `dataDirectory` null means `%ProgramData%\PerformanceMonitorDarling\pg` (inherited ACLs). On shutdown the service stops the server with `pg_ctl stop -m fast` **only if this process started it** â€” a postmaster the operator (or a crashed previous run) owns is adopted for connections, never stopped. A bootstrap failure is LogCritical + clean exit (the existing no-store behavior), each step with an actionable message. The MCP host derives the same string (with a bounded first-boot wait for the credential the worker's initdb writes), and the Viewer's darling.json sliver derives it too, so the whole managed-mode product needs zero connection strings. The plain `connectionString` mode is unchanged. Binaries are NOT committed: the new build-time `Darling/tools/fetch-pg-runtime.ps1` (packaging/CI, never user install time) downloads the pinned EDB PostgreSQL 17.10 Windows binaries + TimescaleDB 2.28.1 zips, verifies their SHA256 pins, assembles `pgsql\{bin,lib,share}` with the Timescale files merged per the proven recipe, and zips `pg-runtime.zip` for shipping beside the service. Pinned ungated (`DarlingManagedPostgresTests` + the config matrix in `DarlingConfigTests`: managed+connectionString = error, derived-string shape, defaults, the conf-append content, password shape, the DPAPI credential round-trip, and the Viewer deriving the identical string from the service-written credential) and end-to-end gated on a NEW variable `DARLING_TEST_PGRUNTIME` (an assembled runtime directory): initdb â†’ start â†’ create db â†’ scram-authenticate into `darling` â†’ idempotent second EnsureRunning (no re-init, no ownership grab) â†’ non-owner stop is a no-op â†’ owner stop really stops â€” into a temp data dir on a scratch port, downloading nothing and touching no real Postgres
- **Darling: scaffold for the net-new headless/centralized edition** ([#1262]) â€” a new top-level `Darling/` path (working name) holds the future 24/7 collector **service** (`PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service`, a .NET Worker with Windows-service lifetime), the detached **viewer** (`PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Viewer`, WPF shell reusing the shared `PerformanceMonitor.Ui` controls), and the **Postgres/TimescaleDB storage layer** (`PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Storage`, Npgsql), plus `Darling.Tests` â€” all wired into the solution and CI. This is milestone M0 of the headless re-architecture: the projects build and run but do nothing yet (the service logs a heartbeat; the viewer shows a placeholder). **The portable Lite edition is architecturally untouched by this path** â€” Darling is net-new code that will share the monitoring brain through the existing `PerformanceMonitor.*` libraries
- **Darling: operator documentation for the headless edition** ([#1262]) â€” a full operator guide at `Darling/README.md` covering the quick start (PostgreSQL prerequisites with optional auto-adopted TimescaleDB, both auth modes with the `--encrypt-password` flow, console mode vs. `sc create` Windows-service installation), what the service creates on monitored servers (the two XE sessions, the blocked-process-threshold bootstrap) and the required permissions, the complete `darling.json` reference with code-verified defaults, the store layout (schema V1â€“V4), retention/compression, the viewer, restart semantics, and troubleshooting â€” plus a Darling introduction in the root README's edition-comparison area
- **Darling: wired into the release pipeline** ([#1262]) â€” release builds now publish the Darling service and viewer (framework-dependent, exactly like Dashboard/Lite), assemble the bundled `pg-runtime.zip` (the pinned EDB PostgreSQL 17.10 + TimescaleDB 2.28.1 runtime, SHA256-verified by `Darling/tools/fetch-pg-runtime.ps1`, downloaded release-only and cached across re-releases keyed on the fetch script's own content hash so unchanged pins skip the ~340MB download), and package one portable `PerformanceMonitorDarling-<version>.zip` â€” the service at the archive root with `pg-runtime.zip` beside its exe (where the first-run bootstrap extracts it) and the viewer in a `viewer\` subfolder â€” checksummed and uploaded to the GitHub release alongside Dashboard/Lite/Installer. Our own Darling exes/dlls join the SignPath signing flow while the EDB PostgreSQL binaries inside `pg-runtime.zip` ship unsigned as opaque data; no Velopack for the service's v1 update story (a Windows service's update story is deliberately deferred). The service and viewer csprojs now carry the shared `<Version>`/`<AssemblyVersion>`/`<FileVersion>` stamp, and the viewer resolves `darling.json` from the service root when it lives in the zip's `viewer\` subfolder â€” the shipped layout works with no extra setup
- **Darling: nightly CI now covers the gated live-PostgreSQL tests** ([#1262]) â€” Darling.Tests' live-Postgres suite (the `[Collection("live-postgres")]` classes plus the managed-bootstrap E2E) is gated on env vars the per-PR/push build never sets, so regular CI exercised only the ungated subset (~169 of ~213). A new `darling-pg` job in the nightly workflow stands up a throwaway PostgreSQL from the bundled runtime â€” the assembled `pg-runtime.zip` restored from the SAME content-hash cache the release build populates (no ~340MB re-download on a warm cache) and extracted, then `initdb`'d with **trust auth** (acceptable only on an ephemeral, loopback-only, throwaway CI runner â€” the product default is the opposite, scram-sha-256 with a generated credential per `DarlingManagedPostgres`) and started on a fixed scratch port with the TimescaleDB preload + loopback bind appended exactly as `DarlingManagedPostgres.BuildConfAppend` writes them, so the Timescale-gated tests light up â€” and runs the full suite with `DARLING_TEST_PG`/`DARLING_TEST_PGRUNTIME` set (validated locally: 213 total, 212 passed, 1 skipped â€” the one live-SQL-Server E2E, which has no SQL Server on the runner). Runs on the existing nightly schedule plus manual dispatch, gated on new commits like the nightly build, and on failure stops the cluster and uploads the PostgreSQL log and test results
- **README: Managed Identity and Service Principal authentication documented** ([#1321]) â€” both have shipped and worked since [#1042]/[#1268] but were never written up; new Authentication section covers the five auth types, how MI/SP map to `Microsoft.Data.SqlClient`'s native `SqlAuthenticationMethod` (no token ever touches app code), and Lite's Credential Profiles for pointing many servers at one shared identity. The Azure SQL Database permissions section gains the matching `CREATE USER ... FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER` grant alongside the existing password-based example
- **Darling: Index Analysis rollup gains the workload-impact columns** ([#1262]) â€” the FinOps Index Analysis reclaimable-space rollup now shows **Reads** (with the seeks/scans/lookups breakdown), **Writes**, **Lock Waits**, **Avg Lock Wait ms**, **Latch Waits**, and **Avg Latch Wait ms** per database, ported from `sp_IndexCleanup`'s `#index_reporting_stats`. The values aggregate the operational lock/latch + usage counters the shared collector already captures into `index_object_stats` (the same data the FinOps Locking sub-tab reads) â€” no collector change. The overall "ALL DATABASES" row renders these as `N/A`, matching `sp_IndexCleanup`'s SUMMARY level. Deferred (a real decision, not built here): the three `Daily *Saved` columns (TABLE-level-only in the proc â€” need a table-level rollup) and the two UDF-metadata columns (`UDF Computed Cols` / `UDF Check Constraints` â€” need new scalar-UDF-reference collection)

### Changed

- **CI: PR build/test/publish is now gated per product, so a change to one edition doesn't rebuild the others** ([#1599]) - the `build` job previously built Lite.Tests + Installer.Tests + Darling AND published all five apps (Dashboard, Lite, Installer, Darling service + viewer) on EVERY non-doc PR, regardless of what changed - the bulk of its wall-clock. Now each product's build/test/publish is gated by a `dorny/paths-filter` that encodes the actual `ProjectReference` graph: a change confined to one product directory (`Lite/`, `Dashboard/`, `Installer/`, `Darling/`) builds only that product; a change to a shared `PerformanceMonitor.*` core library correctly fans out to Lite + Dashboard + Darling (NOT the CLI Installer, which references only `Installer.Core`); `Installer.Core` fans out to Installer + Dashboard; and a root build file (the solution, `global.json`, restore config, or the workflow itself) rebuilds everything. Markdown-only changes under a product dir still short-circuit to a green no-op. Net: a Darling-only PR - the common case - no longer builds/tests Lite or the Installer or publishes the four other apps, and it shrinks further as the Full Dashboard and CLI Installer move to maintenance, since they run CI only when their own code is touched. No test coverage is lost: the four product directories never reference each other (verified via ProjectReference), so a product-scoped change cannot break another product; anything shared triggers the fan-out. Complements the [#1598] gated-live job (unchanged, still Darling-path-gated).

- **CI: the gated-live Darling PostgreSQL test suite now runs on pull requests, not only in the nightly** ([#1598]) - Darling.Tests' live-PostgreSQL tests (the `*_AgainstDevPostgres` classes, gated on `DARLING_TEST_PG`) previously ran ONLY in `nightly.yml`, so a bug they catch could merge AND deploy before the nightly flagged it post-hoc. That is exactly how [#1586]'s `alter_job(bigint)` regression shipped clean through PR CI: the test that proves the live re-arm only ran gated-live. A new `darling-pg` job in `build.yml` stands up a throwaway PostgreSQL + TimescaleDB from the bundled pg-runtime and runs the full Darling suite against it on every PR — but path-filtered to `Darling/**` (and the workflow file itself), so a Lite/Dashboard-only PR does no TimescaleDB work and still reports a green check (the same always-runs-reports-a-result shape the build job uses for doc-only changes), and it can be added to branch protection without blocking unrelated PRs. Mirrors the nightly's proven `darling-pg` job step-for-step and shares its pg-runtime cache key, so a warm cache means no extra download. Closes #1587. NOTE: making it enforce requires adding `Darling PostgreSQL tests` to the required status checks on `dev`/`main` (a branch-protection setting, not a file change).


- **Viewer + Lite: plan-action menu verbs renamed to end the "Actual" overload** ([#1542]) â€” the query-tab context menus offered "View Plan" (the collector's STORED plan), "Fetch Live Plan" (the plan currently in the server's CACHE â€” Darling only), and "Get Actual Plan" (RE-EXECUTES the query with `SET STATISTICS XML`), while the Active Queries grids separately labelled their in-row button + menu item for the DMV-captured runtime plan "Actual" â€” so "Actual" named two different things and two of the three verbs returned an estimated-shape plan, which is exactly why "Get Actual Plan just shows the estimated plan again" kept being reported (the click had landed on View Plan / Fetch Live Plan / the snapshot "Actual", none of which re-execute). The verbs are now unambiguous: **View Stored Plan**, **View Cached Plan**, **Get Actual Plan (re-run)**, and the snapshot runtime plan is **Live** (menu "View Live Plan", in-row button "Live"). Result-tab and message-box titles follow (a Cached-plan result was itself titled "Live Plan" â€” colliding with the snapshot's â€” now "Cached Plan"), as do the blocking/deadlock "Fetch ... Live Plan" verbs (to "View ... Cached Plan"), the FinOps menus, the history/drill windows, and the re-run consent dialog. Labels only â€” no behavior change. Both apps; capability-pin menu-command baselines updated to match.
- **Dashboard: the add/edit-server state machine and its predicates move to `Installer.Core`, where CI can test them** ([#1498]) â€” the `AddServerDialog` flow's state enum (`DialogState`) and its pure decisions â€” is-this-a-verdict-state, does-this-button-promise-an-existing-install, which-state-consumes-the-destructive-checkboxes, and which-connected-state-the-facts-imply â€” lived inside the WPF dialog, which is not wired into CI, so nothing pinned them. Every bug in that flow that survived review came in one of two shapes, and one was "a predicate over the state that silently forgot a state" (the destructive-checkbox rule missing an exit; two predicates disagreeing about the same server). The enum is now `Installer.Core.ServerSetupState` and the predicates are `Installer.Core.ServerSetupPolicy`, pinned by a full stateÃ—predicate matrix in `Installer.Tests` (CI-wired) that asserts every predicate for every state â€” so a switch that forgets a state fails a test instead of only misbehaving in the UI. **Behavior-preserving:** the dialog aliases the enum as `DialogState` and calls the extracted predicates, no logic changed; all 732 Dashboard tests pass unchanged. This is the testability follow-up the multi-round review of this PR kept pointing at.
- **Darling + Lite: the `system_health` significance predicates are collapsed into one shared copy in `PerformanceMonitor.Common` (Tier-1 parity groundwork)** ([#1507]) â€” sp_HealthParser's per-category "significant / warning" predicates (WARNING scheduler status, severity >= 19 severe errors excluding the benign reset numbers, RESOURCE_MEMPHYSICAL_LOW memory-conditions/broker notifications, the `pendingTasks >= 10` CPU-task gate, the IO_SUBSYSTEM WARNING gate, the always-on 500 ms significant-wait predicate with its 90-entry idle-wait ignore list, and the ungated memory-node-OOM) were **hand-duplicated** in the Darling viewer's `SystemEventSignificance` and the headless MCP host's `DarlingSystemHealthSignificance`, and the two copies had **ALREADY drifted** â€” the service copy was missing the significant-wait predicate, its wait-duration constant, and the entire idle-wait ignore list. They now live once in `PerformanceMonitor.Common.SystemHealthSignificance` (the same one-source pattern `DefaultTraceEventSignificance` already uses); the viewer facade `SystemEventSignificance` **delegates** (keeping its full public surface, so viewer call sites and tests are untouched) and the service twin is **deleted** with its seven MCP-tool call sites repointed to the shared class. **Behavior-preserving** â€” the existing anti-drift equivalence tests and fixture round-trips stay green (84 passed, 0 failed), now pinning the shared class and guarding that the viewer facade still delegates instead of growing a third copy. Groundwork for the Lite System Events tab, so Lite consumes the shared predicates rather than becoming that third hand-maintained copy. Scoped to Common + Darling â€” the deprecated Full Dashboard (whose significance is server-side sp_HealthParser) is untouched. Darling + Common (Lite<->Darling parity groundwork)
- **Lite and Darling: the triplicated baseline analysis MODEL is collapsed into one shared copy in `PerformanceMonitor.Analysis` (Tier 0 parity collapse)** ([#1503]) â€” the last Tier-0 forcing-function item. The anomaly brain's baseline MODEL was carried VERBATIM in three places (Lite `BaselineProvider`/`AnomalyDetector`, Darling `PgBaselineProvider`/`PgAnomalyDetector`, Dashboard `SqlServer*`) â€” a drift risk the code itself flagged in `AnomalyGate`. The shared pieces: the `BaselineBucket` model (mean/stddev/sample-count/distinct-days + `EffectiveStdDev`/`IsTrustworthy` and the six tier-gate floors), the `BaselineTier` enum, the store-agnostic `BaselineMath` (tier selection with hysteresis, hour-only/flat pooling, pooled variance, the bounded-metric dispersion floor, and the rolling-window/collapse/restore thresholds), the ~20 anomaly-detector tuning constants in `AnomalyThresholds` (deviation/ratio thresholds, the #1486 magnitude floors, the low-quality-baseline absolute-fallback bars, the sigma cap, the object-growth thresholds), and the `MetricNames` cache keys. Verified byte-identical between Lite and Darling FIRST, then extracted to shared types that BOTH active apps reference; each provider keeps only the genuinely dialect-specific part â€” its per-metric DuckDB/Postgres baseline SQL + parameter binding. **Behavior-preserving:** not one threshold, constant, or line of logic changed â€” the full Lite and Darling baseline/anomaly suites pass unchanged (the Lite tier-collapse integration tests and the Darling QUALIFY-rewrite / `EffectiveStdDev` pins exercise the extracted math). Scoped to the two ACTIVE apps: the deprecated Full Dashboard keeps its OWN frozen copy untouched (a bug on a frozen app is unacceptable risk), and the shared types live in the `PerformanceMonitor.Analysis.Baselines` sub-namespace precisely so a plain `using PerformanceMonitor.Analysis;` in the Dashboard's files can't collide with its own same-named `BaselineBucket`/`BaselineTier` (CS0104) â€” the Dashboard compiles unchanged. Adds a mirror-image FORCING-FUNCTION pin on each side (`SharedBaselineModelPinTests` in Lite.Tests and Darling.Tests) asserting each app binds the model to the shared assembly and holds NO per-app copy, so Lite and Darling can't silently re-fork it again. Lite + Darling (Lite<->Darling parity collapse)
- **Lite: the 35 collector-table DuckDB schemas are now GENERATED from the shared `CollectorCatalog` (Tier 0 parity collapse) instead of hand-written** ([#1502]) â€” Lite used to hand-write 35 `CREATE TABLE` constants + 33 index constants in `Schema.cs`, plus TWO parallel hand-maintained archive lists (`DuckDbInitializer.ArchivableTables`, `ArchiveService.ArchivableTables`) â€” the documented "three registrations" drift trap â€” while Darling GENERATES the identical collector schema from `CollectorCatalog` and pins it column-for-column. That asymmetry was the single largest place Lite's store could silently diverge from Darling's. A new `DuckDbSchemaGenerator` (the DuckDB twin of Darling's `PgSchemaGenerator`) now walks `CollectorCatalog.All` and emits each collector's `CREATE TABLE`/`CREATE INDEX`; `Schema.cs` keeps only the 7 non-collector tables (registry / schedule / log / alert-coordination), and both archive lists derive from the catalog. A collector column added in one place now flows to Lite, Darling, AND the archive/purge machinery automatically. **This is a storage-schema change gated on a hard equivalence proof, not a refactor taken on faith:** the generated DDL is demonstrated byte-for-byte STORAGE-equivalent to the pre-generation hand-written tables for all 35 tables (`DuckDbSchemaEquivalenceTests` executes both the frozen pre-change DDL and the freshly generated DDL against DuckDB and compares `PRAGMA table_info` row-for-row â€” identical columns, DuckDB types, order, NOT NULL, DEFAULT, PRIMARY KEY â€” plus matching indexes). The two things the engine-neutral catalog does not carry but Lite's DuckDB schema has always enforced â€” the prefix `PRIMARY KEY` and NOT NULL on the always-populated key/name/count payload columns, plus the lone `query_snapshots.is_cdc_capture DEFAULT false` (Darling deliberately drops both for its hypertable/COPY path) â€” are reproduced exactly by a small, pinned, Lite-local constraint overlay, and Lite's historical irregular index names (`idx_cpu_time`, `idx_query_store_time`, the composite `idx_index_object_stats_object`, `memory_pressure_events` on `sample_time`, â€¦) are preserved verbatim so an existing store's `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` matches rather than adding a redundant index. **Existing DuckDB stores are unaffected**: every statement is `CREATE ... IF NOT EXISTS` (a no-op on an already-created table), the 45-step migration ladder and `CurrentSchemaVersion` are untouched, so the generator only shapes FRESH stores â€” and it shapes them identically to before. Adds a FORCING-FUNCTION pin (`DuckDbSchemaGeneratorTests`) asserting every generated table's columns + types equal the catalog definition, so a catalog change must flow to Lite and no hand-edit can silently diverge. Full Lite.Tests green (1125 tests incl. every collector round-trip, which exercises the positional appender against the generated schema). Lite (Lite<->Darling parity groundwork)
- **Analysis findings: parallelism (CXPACKET) can no longer reach CRITICAL on amplifier stacking alone; a real thread-exhaustion meltdown escalates through THREADPOOL instead (Stage B of the recs-engine remainder â€” the severity half)** ([#1494]) â€” on the shared `FactScorer`, so all three apps (Lite, Dashboard, Darling) change identically. **(A) Tuning-class severity cap:** CXPACKET saturates its base severity to 1.0 at 25%-of-period, and its corroborating amplifiers (default CTFP, MAXDOP 0, high-DOP queries) then multiplied it past the shared `>= 1.5` CRITICAL band on amplifier COUNT alone â€” so a pure parallelism-tuning situation e-mailed as CRITICAL. A new Layer-3 pass caps the FINAL severity of the tuning-class keys (CXPACKET / CXCONSUMER / QUERY_HIGH_DOP + every `ANOMALY_*`) at the 1.49 WARNING ceiling, WITH an escape hatch: the cap is released the moment an impact-bearing peer co-fires WITH REAL WEIGHT â€” THREADPOOL thread exhaustion (whose base is self-gating: `ScoreWaitFact` only scores it at `>= 1hr` total AND `>= 1s` avg wait, so its `BaseSeverity > 0` already means exhaustion), a SIGNIFICANT SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD (`>= 25%` of period, CPU starvation), or a SIGNIFICANT RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE (`>= 10%` of period, grant starvation) â€” because then the parallelism genuinely IS driving an outage and CRITICAL is earned. The SOS/RS bars gate on the same `HasSignificantWait` fraction-of-period the CXPACKET amplifiers use, NOT on mere `BaseSeverity > 0`: unlike THREADPOOL, neither SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD (0.75, null) nor RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE (0.01, null) has a minimum-total guard, so `BaseSeverity > 0` there fires on ANY trace of the wait â€” and SOS physically co-occurs with high CXPACKET (parallel workers yield â†’ SOS) and is emitted for any `delta_wait_time_ms > 0`, so a trivial SOS (e.g. 500ms over an hour) with the presence-only check would release the cap on exactly the busy servers it targets, re-admitting the CXPACKET=CRITICAL noise the cap exists to kill (SOS 0.25 matches the CXPACKET SOS amplifier's bar; RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE has no amplifier bar, so 0.10 was chosen â€” 10% of period is meaningful grant starvation). It caps the numeric `Severity` only (the band is derived downstream), so a capped fact stays WARNING without a separate band field and cross-app parity is preserved. Reality-check: CXPACKET is the only key with teeth here today â€” `ANOMALY_*` and QUERY_HIGH_DOP already max at 1.0 â€” so the rest of the set is forward-safety as those ramps evolve. **(D) Impact escalation:** so a genuine parallelism â†’ worker-exhaustion meltdown still reaches CRITICAL, THREADPOOL (an impact key, never capped) gets a heavier CXPACKET amplifier (+0.2 â†’ +0.5) plus a new **runnable-queue amplifier** (+0.5) that fires off a `RUNNABLE_TASKS` context fact read from the EXISTING `cpu_scheduler_stats` snapshot's `runnable_tasks_warning` flag (the collector's own `SUM(runnable_tasks_count) >= cpu_count` heuristic) â€” NO new collector, table, or migration; it reuses data both SKUs already store. That read is window-bounded to the analysis period in both providers (a lower `collection_time >= start` bound added to the prior `<= end`-only `ORDER BY â€¦ DESC LIMIT 1` snapshot, matching sibling `CpuUtilizationSql`), so a lapsed collection can no longer surface an arbitrarily old snapshot from outside the window â€” the fact is then absent and the amplifier no-ops; the SQL stays byte-identical between the Lite (DuckDB) and Darling (Postgres) collectors. The `RUNNABLE_TASKS` fact is surfaced by the Lite and Darling fact collectors (Source `cpu`, carried into the amplifier lookup but never scored/rooted, like every `cpu`-source key besides CPU_SQL_PERCENT/CPU_SPIKE); the deprecated Dashboard's fact collection is deliberately left untouched, where the amplifier simply no-ops â€” the shared cap + the raised CXPACKET amplifier still escalate a Dashboard meltdown to CRITICAL through THREADPOOL. `cpu_scheduler_stats` isn't collected on Azure SQL DB, so the fact is absent there too and the amplifier no-ops. **(B) Confidence-hardening:** the Stage-A low-quality anomaly fallback now floors at 0.5 AFTER the confidence multiply (`Math.Max(0.5, (â€¦) * confidence)`) instead of before it â€” behaviorally identical today (confidence is a hardcoded 1.0 everywhere) but robust to a future sub-1.0 confidence that would otherwise silently drop a fired thin-baseline finding below `InferenceEngine`'s 0.5 root entry-point. Blocking/deadlock detection stays on its current raw-count behavior (unchanged). Ships with new `FactScorerTests` in both Lite.Tests and Dashboard.Tests (CXPACKET alone caps to WARNING; CXPACKET + THREADPOOL releases the cap into CRITICAL; a TRIVIAL SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD or RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE below the significance bar leaves CXPACKET capped at WARNING, while a SIGNIFICANT one releases it into CRITICAL â€” the regression that would have caught the presence-only escape hatch; THREADPOOL reaches CRITICAL via the raised amplifier + the runnable-queue amplifier; the runnable-queue amplifier does not fire when the warning is clear; the confidence floor holds at 0.5 under a hypothetical sub-1.0 confidence). Lite + Dashboard + Darling
- **Analysis findings: anomaly detections fold into the regular incident, and alerts dedup per incident (Stage B of the recs-engine remainder)** ([#1493]) â€” an `ANOMALY_*` detection and the regular finding for the same symptom (e.g. an `ANOMALY_WAIT_PROFILE` dominated by CX* waits and the CXPACKET "Parallelism waits" finding, or `ANOMALY_CPU_SPIKE` and `CPU_SQL_PERCENT`) stopped rendering as two separate cards and firing two separate e-mails for one event. A new shared `AnomalyIncidentReconciler` runs after incident clustering in all three analysis services (Darling, Lite, Dashboard) and, for each surviving anomaly story, rewrites its stamped incident id onto the regular finding of the same symptom family â€” matched wherever that family fact sits in the parent story (its root OR any mid/leaf member it consumed, so an `ANOMALY_CPU_SPIKE` still folds into an `SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD`-rooted CPU story that merely *contains* `CPU_SQL_PERCENT` or the burst `CPU_SPIKE`, which is the common correlated case where dedup matters most), and only when that parent exists in the **same run and same database**; a no-parent anomaly stays solo, and a db-scoped `ANOMALY_OBJECT_*` never folds across databases. The family map mirrors the collector's own wait grouping exactly (`FactCollectorHelpers.WaitFamilyKey`: CX* â†’ CXPACKET, general lock modes â†’ LCK, with `LCK_M_S` / range / schema locks kept separate), and `ANOMALY_WAIT_PROFILE` resolves to the family of its dominant `contrib_<TYPE>` driver. This is fold-not-suppress â€” nothing is dropped, only the incident tag is reconciled, so the anomaly's vs-baseline context and any sub-threshold or no-regular-path signal are preserved. `AnalysisNotificationService` groups by `incident_id` (falling back to the story-path hash for legacy/absolution findings that have no id, so those never collapse together) and sends **one alert per incident per cycle** â€” led by the highest-severity member whose re-notify bucket is not cooling and naming the co-fired rest in that single message via the shared `CoFiredSummary` â€” but **escalates on CRITICAL**: a member at or above the shared `>= 1.5` CRITICAL severity cutoff (the same band the Dashboard/Lite/Darling recommendation readers use) is cooled on its OWN per-finding story-path-hash bucket instead of the incident bucket, so a NEW critical symptom joining an already-alerted incident sends a fresh e-mail naming that critical â€” never silently held under the incident cooldown â€” while below-critical co-fired symptoms stay deduped one-per-incident. Each cycle still collapses simultaneously co-firing symptoms into a single message (send-if-any-bucket-fresh, stamp-all, the same rule the live `IncidentCooldown` path uses); a lone critical e-mails once and respects its own cooldown, and two genuinely distinct incidents never collapse. Also fixes a latent cross-database clustering bug: `InferenceEngine.ClusterIntoIncidents` no longer unions two db-scoped findings from DIFFERENT databases into one incident (server-scoped findings still bridge freely, so same-database and server-wide incidents are unaffected).
- **Darling: `collect.collection_log` becomes a TimescaleDB hypertable â€” O(1) `drop_chunks` retention + compression, scale-readiness for the up-to-500-servers store** ([#1479]) â€” `collection_log` (the per-collector-run observability log) is the store's highest-volume plain table (~123k rows / 17 MB at 5-server scale) and was a heap with insert + a daily batched-DELETE purge, full-scanned every Overview refresh by the per-server `MAX(collection_time)` freshness read. It is now a TimescaleDB hypertable (schema **V23**): retention becomes an O(1) `drop_chunks` metadata drop instead of DELETE churn (`DarlingRetention`, with a batched-DELETE fallback so a plain-PostgreSQL store â€” or a table whose conversion failed â€” still honors the 60-day horizon), the freshness read hits only the newest chunk per server via the kept `(server_id, collection_time)` index, and old chunks compress (segment by `server_id`, compress-after 1 day) as the archival tier. Like the [#1478] memory tuning this is scale-readiness, **not** a fix for observed pressure (autovacuum keeps up at 5-server scale). The conversion is applied AUTHORITATIVELY at runtime by `TimescaleSupport.EnsureCollectionLogHypertableAsync` â€” the same proven-live, non-transactional, failure-isolated path every collector table uses â€” deliberately NOT solely by the V23 migration, because migrations run BEFORE `CREATE EXTENSION timescaledb` (so a fresh store's migration-time extension guard is false) and inside the startup-critical migration transaction (a `create_hypertable(migrate_data)` failure there would brick startup); the V23 migration carries a guarded, `EXCEPTION`-safe best-effort conversion as an upgrade fast-path, and yields to the runtime path otherwise. `collection_log` lives outside `CollectorCatalog.All`, so the managed-Postgres `max_background_workers` sizing now derives from `TimescaleSupport.HypertableCount` (catalog + 1) so its compression policy is not under-provisioned, and the viewer's connect-time schema-skew probe gains a plain-PostgreSQL-safe V23 sentinel (`collection_log` is a hypertable via the core `pg_trigger` `ts_insert_blocker` catalog â€” never the extension-only `timescaledb_information` view, which would throw on plain PG â€” OR the store has no `timescaledb`, where V23 is an object-invisible no-op) so a correctly-migrated store is never falsely gated. Plain PostgreSQL remains fully supported (the conversion no-ops; DELETE-based retention stands).
- **Darling viewer W2b: the Recommendations aggregate tab re-skinned to Lite's advise-only card design** ([#1262]) â€” the finding grid + read-only detail pane become a header (its OWN server selector, independent of the sidebar; a Refresh button; a status line showing the last analysis time) over a scrollable list of collapsible **incident** sections, each holding severity-banded cards (a severity badge, the affected `[database]`, the title, and the advice) â€” the same shaping as Lite's `RecommendationsTab`, grouped by `incident_id`, reads unchanged on the existing `analysis_findings` path. Every card offers **Ask AI** (copies the MCP investigation prompt ported verbatim from Lite, referencing `analyze_server` / `get_analysis_findings`); a card whose finding's persisted remediation carries a copy-paste statement also offers **Copy fix** (the suggested T-SQL, rebuilt from the stored `remediation_action_json` the way the Dashboard reader derives it on read-back, since the ephemeral drill-down `FactRemediation.GenerateForFinding` needs is not persisted). Advise-only: no Apply, and the finding **mute/unmute context menu + Muted column were removed** â€” mute moves to the Alert History surface per Lite; the `ViewerDataService` mute read/write API and its tests are retained for that side. There is no in-app "Generate now" (the service runs analysis on its own 30-minute cadence): the tab refreshes on activation, its Refresh button, and its own server-selector change only â€” never the 60-second timer â€” and the status line surfaces the last analysis time instead. Ungated tests pin the incident grouping, the card content (Copy-fix payload, Ask-AI prompt text), and the severity banding; a gated live-Postgres round-trip proves the persisted-action to Copy-fix path.
- **Lite: `date_diff` replaced with the PostgreSQL-shared spelling, bit-exactly (headless groundwork, phase 1a)** ([#1262]) â€” PostgreSQL has no `date_diff` function, and the naive `EXTRACT(EPOCH ...)` substitution is subtly different (elapsed time vs. DuckDB's boundary-crossing count â€” off by one around second/day boundaries). All 11 sites now use forms proven **value-identical** by adversarial probe: the ten per-collection rate denominators (`interval_seconds` via `LAG`) become `extract(epoch FROM (date_trunc('second', a) - date_trunc('second', b)))` (truncate-then-diff = exact boundary count; the one reader-exposed site keeps its BIGINT type via CAST), and the FinOps `days_of_data` becomes plain `DATE` subtraction (`CAST(max AS DATE) - CAST(min AS DATE)`), which both engines define as whole day-boundary counts. Probe also caught a portability trap avoided here: DuckDB's `date_trunc('day', ts)` returns a `DATE` where PostgreSQL returns a timestamp, so the day site deliberately avoids `date_trunc`
- **Lite: DuckDB SQL normalized to the PostgreSQL-shared dialect (headless groundwork, phase 1a)** ([#1262]) â€” every `DOUBLE` type spelling in Lite's DuckDB SQL becomes the standard `DOUBLE PRECISION` (43 `::DOUBLE` casts, 90 `CAST(... AS DOUBLE)`, schema/ALTER DDL â€” DuckDB aliases the two spellings to the same type, so existing databases, values, and introspection are unchanged), and a three-way audit of all 257 SQL division sites hardened the 8 that relied on implicit engine-specific promotion (execution-weighted per-exec averages in the analysis engine gain an explicit `::DOUBLE PRECISION` numerator; FinOps workload CPU-ms sums divide by `1000.0` instead of `1000`, matching their siblings). The remaining 162 DuckDB divisions were verified already float-safe, and the 87 divisions in collector T-SQL are intentionally untouched (SQL Server semantics, including intended integer truncation). No behavior change on DuckDB â€” groundwork so the same query text can later serve the planned Postgres/TimescaleDB headless store
- **Darling viewer: the Latches & Spinlocks tab is consolidated into one view** ([#1488]) â€” the tab previously split its two contention views across inner sub-tabs (Latch Stats / Spinlock Stats), each a full-height trend chart stacked over a latest-snapshot grid. The two per-second trend charts (latch wait-time, spinlock collisions) now stack vertically on a single tab, and each per-class latest-snapshot grid moves into a collapsed `Expander` directly beneath its chart â€” both trends are visible at once and the tab is decluttered by default, with the per-class detail one click away. Every grid column, binding, and the chart window-pinning (`SetLimitsX` after `DateTimeTicksBottomDateChange`) are preserved verbatim. No reader change: the investigation into "the grids don't seem to be doing anything" confirmed the grids were binding their store data correctly â€” the snapshot reads are type-correct against the generated Postgres schema (`spins_per_collision` is `double precision`, every counter `bigint`) and cannot return empty when the trend chart has rows (same window, latest-collection logic); the impression was the cramped bottom-third grid, which this decluttering addresses

### Removed

- **Plan Cache tab: dropped the momentary Cache Object Type breakdown grid (both apps)** ([#1539]) â€” a time-series chart doesn't need a point-in-time aggregate grid beneath it; kept the over-time single-use vs multi-use size chart plus the Total Plans / Oldest Plan Age / Bloat Level summary strip. Removed the `PlanCacheCompositionGrid` from both the Lite `ServerTab` and the Darling viewer's `ViewerServerTab` (the Plan Cache tab's row layout collapses from chart / summary / grid to chart-over-summary), the grid's Lite filter-manager (`_planCacheCompositionFilterMgr`) and its copy-header registration, and the matching capability-pin baseline lines (Lite `servertab-filtermgrs` + `servertab-gridstyle`, Darling `viewerservertab-gridstyle`). The `PlanCacheSnapshotRow` type and the `GetPlanCacheSnapshotAsync` reader are RETAINED in both apps â€” Lite's `get_plan_cache_bloat` MCP tool and the Darling `ViewerPlanCacheTests` snapshot round-trip still consume them. The deprecated Full Dashboard has no such tab and is untouched.
- **Queries > Expensive Queries sub-tab dropped from both apps; the Performance Calendar day-drill repoints to Top Queries** ([#1544]) â€” the Queries tab's unified "Expensive Queries" grid (one cross-source list UNIONing Query Stats + Stored Procedures/Triggers/Functions + Query Store, ranked by average CPU) bought nothing the tab's surviving sub-tabs don't already give: Top Queries, Query Store, and Top Procedures cover the same resource consumers with per-source time-range slicers, current-vs-baseline comparison, and per-row history. It is removed from both the Lite `ServerTab` and the Darling viewer's `ViewerServerTab` â€” the `<TabItem Header="Expensive Queries">` and its `ExpensiveQueriesGrid`, the whole per-tab reader (Lite `UnifiedExpensiveQueryRow` + `GetUnifiedExpensiveQueriesAsync` in `LocalDataService.ExpensiveQueries.cs`; Darling `ViewerExpensiveQueryRow` + `UnifiedExpensiveQueriesSql` + the `ExpensiveSource*` labels in `ViewerDataService.ExpensiveQueries.cs`, both files deleted), the lazy/eager load paths, the grid's filter manager, and its arms in the View-Plan / View-Cached-Plan / Copy-Repro-Script handlers. In Darling the Query Heatmap sub-tab index renumbers down by one (8 â†’ 7) so the sub-tab indices stay contiguous. **The Performance Calendar's always-available day-drill is repointed, not orphaned:** the shared `DayDrillTarget.ExpensiveQueries` enum member is honestly renamed `TopQueries`, its day-detail button becomes "View Top Queries", and both hosts' drill handlers now select and load the **Top Queries** sub-tab for the clicked day (Lite already navigated there; Darling previously selected the now-gone Expensive grid). The reader tests for the dropped grids are deleted, the shared `DailyHealthBand` drill tests and the Darling database-filter pin are updated, and the capability-pin tab / grid-style / filter-manager baselines drop their Expensive Queries lines in both apps. **The FinOps tab's own Expensive Queries grid is a different surface and is untouched**, as is the deprecated Full Dashboard.

### Fixed

- **Darling.Tests: the compression self-heal gated-live test no longer races the TimescaleDB scheduler** ([#1604]) - the `CompressionJobSelfHeal` `*_AgainstDevPostgres` test asserts a freshly-applied compression policy job is NOT flagged as stuck, but a just-added TimescaleDB job can momentarily read `next_start = '-infinity'` in `job_stats` BEFORE its background scheduler assigns the first real next run - and the stuck-job detector is CORRECT to flag `-infinity` - so asserting immediately after `ApplyCompressionPolicy` raced that window and intermittently false-failed on a slow CI runner. **Fix:** deterministically settle the job into the healthy state the assertion is about - arm it with a real future `next_start` (via the same `alter_job` the self-heal uses), then wait for the catalog to reflect a non-`(-infinity)` `next_start` before the "not flagged" assertion, bounded to 30s and failing loudly if it never settles (a job that stays `-infinity` genuinely IS stuck and must not silently pass). Test-only; the detector (`ReadStuckCompressionJobsAsync`) and the self-heal path are unchanged. Follows the [#1585]/[#1586]/[#1588] compression self-heal work.

- **Darling service: the sweep hang-watchdog no longer cries wolf when a server is merely waiting its turn** ([#1597]) - field follow-up to [#1590]. After the cold-start stagger fixed the restart herd, the field box still logged **82 "collection body has not completed after 60s" warnings in 51 minutes** (~1.6/min, down from ~5.6/min), with multiple servers warning at the SAME timestamp — a shared-contention signature, not per-server slowness. Root cause, verified in source rather than inferred: `SweepStartedUtc` is stamped at LAUNCH, so GATE QUEUE TIME counted toward the 60s watchdog. With 24 servers behind the hardcoded `MaxConcurrentServerSweeps = 4` gate, any tick where more than four servers fall due queues the excess, and a body whose wait crossed 60s logged a hang warning **despite never having started executing**. The [#1590] stagger deliberately gates only the FIRST launch (`InFlightSweep is null`), so steady-state cadence alignment was never covered — this was pre-existing at that fleet size, not a regression from [#1590] or [#1593]. **Why it mattered:** that warning is the HANG detector ("the field incident was HANGS, not throws"), and at ~82 false alarms an hour a genuine stall was invisible in its own channel. **Fix:** split the episode into QUEUED vs RUNNING. The body stamps `RunStartedTicks` when it acquires the concurrency gate — the one sweep-bookkeeping field the body writes, via `Interlocked`, deliberately separate so the three outer-thread-only fields keep their [#1553] fire-and-track invariant untouched — and the watchdog then judges a RUNNING body on its own execution clock (Warning: the real stall) and a QUEUED body on the episode clock (Information: "waited Ns for a free slot ... queued, not stalled"), each latched to fire once per episode. `SweepStartedUtc` still spans the whole episode, because a queued server genuinely IS unserved; that fact is kept, just routed to the channel where it belongs. The decision is extracted as a pure `ClassifySweepEpisode` so the truth table is unit-pinned — including the exact field shape (queued ~5 minutes, then ran 3 seconds → silent, where the old measurement called it a hang) and that a ten-minute queue can never reach the hang channel. `MaxConcurrentServerSweeps` is deliberately NOT raised: that trades against the memory bounds won in [#1557]/[#1581] and is the wrong lever for an alignment problem. Verified: full `Darling.Tests` **2750 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped**.

- **Darling service: settled the #1581 "narrow the credential ACL scope" question — the lockdown does NOT silence file logging, and the scope stays** ([#1596]) - #1581 carried an open decision: the service hardens `ParentOf(dataDirectory)` (`%ProgramData%\PerformanceMonitorDarling`), which ALSO contains the service's own `logs` directory, so the credential lockdown was suspected of making logs unwritable and "narrow the ACL scope" was held as a pending security call. Settled by investigation + a real-I/O test rather than a code change: `DarlingFileSecurity.HardenDirectory` grants `WindowsIdentity.GetCurrent().User` — the identity the service is ACTUALLY running as, not a hardcoded SID — FullControl with `ContainerInherit|ObjectInherit`, so `logs` INHERITS write access and a running service can never lock itself out of its own log directory. The new test proves on disk that after hardening the parent exactly as the service does it: an existing log file is still appendable, a newly rotated log file can be created and written, and a data-dir subtree created AFTERWARDS is writable including `server.key` — while confirming the lockdown genuinely did reach both (no Everyone / Authenticated Users / Users ACE survives on either). **No production change, deliberately:** a field log-ACL failure therefore means something EXTERNAL re-ACL'd the path (on the field box, a SYSTEM-context SSM operation that reset one specific log file's ACL while the parent directory's grant was still correct), and narrowing the hardening off the parent would drop the inherited protection from the data-directory subtree — including the Postgres TLS private key `server.key` — in exchange for nothing. The test exists so that trade is not made later by mistake.

- **Darling service: removing an already-absent firewall rule no longer logs a bogus warning on every shutdown** ([#1594]) - field validation on two independent hosts surfaced `Could not remove the firewall rule automatically (exit 1: )` - note the EMPTY message - at store/MCP shutdown. The root cause is a PowerShell exit-code trap, not a firewall problem: `BuildFirewallDisableCommand` used `Remove-NetFirewallRule -DisplayName '...' -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue`, and `SilentlyContinue` suppresses the error OUTPUT while the cmdlet failure still makes `powershell.exe` exit 1. So the COMMON no-op - removing a rule that was never created, or that a prior shutdown already removed - reported failure with nothing to show for it, on every host, every shutdown. Reproduced empirically (exit 1, category `ObjectNotFound`, `CmdletizationQuery_NotFound_DisplayName`). **Fix:** an absent rule IS this command's desired end state, so it is now `try { Remove-NetFirewallRule ... -ErrorAction Stop } catch { if ($_.CategoryInfo.Category -ne 'ObjectNotFound') { throw } }; exit 0` - ObjectNotFound is swallowed and exits 0, while any OTHER failure (e.g. access denied, which leaves a stale allow rule behind and is worth knowing about) rethrows so the caller still warns, now with a real message. The trailing `exit 0` is load-bearing: merely CATCHING the error still leaves a non-zero exit state (the catch-only form was verified to still exit 1). A single builder serves both call sites - `DarlingHostBinding` reuses it - so the store rule (5641) and the MCP/Web host bindings are all fixed at once. The enable command is deliberately untouched: its trailing `New-NetFirewallRule` determines the exit code, so its idempotent leading remove never mis-reports. A regression test pins the shape (no `SilentlyContinue`, `-ErrorAction Stop`, ObjectNotFound guard, rethrow, `exit 0`).

- **FinOps Server Inventory: an Azure SQL DB monitoring login without VIEW DATABASE STATE no longer loses the entire server row** ([#1592]) - a follow-up to [#1589], which fixed the connectivity probe but explicitly left the FinOps inventory *display* queries (`DatabaseService.FinOps.Inventory` / `LocalDataService.FinOps.ServerProperties`) untouched. Those `GetServerPropertiesLiveAsync` queries selected `SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')`, the edition CASE, and the storage/host facts in the SAME `SELECT ... FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info` that also read the hardware columns (`cpu_count`, `physical_memory_kb`, `sqlserver_start_time`, `socket_count`, `cores_per_socket`). On Azure SQL DB that DMV requires VIEW DATABASE STATE; a monitoring login without it made the WHOLE query throw, so the FinOps **Server Inventory** grid dropped the server's ENTIRE row (edition, version, storage - everything) and the tab only wrote an `AppLogger.Error`, leaving the operator with an empty inventory and no idea why. **Fix (both Lite and the deprecated Full Dashboard):** each `GetServerPropertiesLiveAsync` is split into two pinned `const` queries. `InventoryQueryText` keeps every permission-free fact - the edition CASE, the `SERVERPROPERTY` scalars, and the engine-edition-aware dynamic-SQL storage read - with the five hardware columns replaced by typed NULLs and NO `FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info`, so it succeeds for any connected login; the command+reader are scoped closed before the second read opens (the connection has no MARS). `HardwareQueryText` (`SELECT cpu_count, physical_memory_kb / 1024, sqlserver_start_time, socket_count, cores_per_socket FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info`) then runs in its OWN try/catch and overwrites the hardware fields on success; on failure it sets a new `HardwareUnavailableReason` and the row still renders edition/version/storage. The Dashboard's inventory storage additionally stopped hard-coding `sys.master_files` (absent on Azure SQL DB) - it now uses the same `EngineEdition = 5 ? sys.database_files : sys.master_files` branch Lite already had, so the Dashboard inventory query is Azure-safe end to end. **Visibility:** the FinOps Server Inventory grid in both apps gains a non-alarming amber "Hardware Note" column beside the CPU/memory/socket fields that shows the reason (full text on hover) only when hardware inventory was denied; the Dashboard MCP `get_server_properties` tool also returns `hardware_unavailable_reason`. A regression-guard test class in each app pins the split (InventoryQueryText carries no `dm_os_sys_info` and still carries `SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')`; HardwareQueryText carries `sys.dm_os_sys_info`), and the README documents that VIEW DATABASE STATE is what enables the `sys.dm_os_*` hardware inventory on Azure SQL DB. Verified: the full solution builds clean (0 errors); Lite.Tests 1434 passed / 0 failed, and the new Dashboard.Tests inventory-query class 3 passed / 0 failed. No Darling code changed (Darling.Tests 2740 passed / 142 gated-live skipped locally; the one local failure is a pre-existing timezone-sensitive viewer test, green on UTC CI).

- **Darling analysis: the RegressedQueries plan-regression drill-down no longer scans all Query Store history on large stores** ([#1593]) - a field box reported `PgDrillDownCollector.CollectRegressedQueries` taking ~6.3s. Root cause: the query filtered `query_store_stats` by `last_execution_time`, which is NOT the hypertable partition key (`collection_time` is), so TimescaleDB could not exclude chunks and scanned the ENTIRE retained history, decompressing every chunk, then filtered - cost that grows without bound as history accumulates. **Fix:** add a provably-safe `collection_time >= window - interval '1 day'` bound (a row is always collected at/after its interval's last execution, so `last_execution_time >= window` already implies it; the day of slack is safety), letting TimescaleDB exclude whole chunks; replace the `ranked` self-join with two `DISTINCT ON (database_name, query_id)` reads (latest-by-exec, cheapest-by-cpu) so the `plan_dedup` CTE materializes ONCE instead of the pipeline running per join side; and collapse the redundant `plan_agg`+`plan_dedup` two-stage aggregate into one (a weighted-average of weighted-averages equals the direct execution_count-weighted average). The load-bearing per-interval `deduped` step (keep only the LATEST snapshot per Query Store interval) is KEPT - `query_store_stats` rows are cumulative per-interval snapshots that the incremental collector re-fetches with a growing `execution_count`, so removing it would multiply-count executions; Postgres already optimizes it via a `Run Condition` window (`row_number() <= 1`), so it stays cheap. Also pins a 30s `CommandTimeout` on every `PgDrillDownCollector` query (all run inside the 60s DarlingWorker sweep budget). Verified against real TimescaleDB on the DARLING01 test box: the rewrite is behaviorally identical to the original (same regressions, same recommended best plans, on the busiest 28k-query server) and runs ~440ms there; the chunk-exclusion win is what collapses the multi-second time on a large store.

- **Connectivity probe: Azure SQL DB is no longer silently mis-detected as on-prem when the monitoring login lacks VIEW DATABASE STATE** ([#1589]) - all three SKUs (Lite, the deprecated Full Dashboard, and Darling) probed the target with `CONVERT(integer, SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition'))` selected in the SAME `SELECT ... FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info`. On Azure SQL DB that DMV requires VIEW DATABASE STATE; a monitoring login without it makes the WHOLE probe throw (`Msg 262 / VIEW DATABASE STATE permission denied`), and the catch kept the server "online" but left `SqlEngineEdition = 0`, so `IsAzureSqlDb = (edition == 5)` read FALSE and every collector then ran its ON-PREM query - erroring on `dm_os_sys_memory`, `sys.traces`, `dm_xe_session_targets`, and `msdb`. Proven on a real Azure SQL DB: as a full admin the probe returns edition 5, but as a permission-starved login the probe throws WHILE `SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')` on its own still returns 5 (SERVERPROPERTY needs no permission) - so platform detection must not depend on that DMV. **Fix:** each probe is split into two reads. A DETECTION query with NO `FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info` - `SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')`, `SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMajorVersion')`, `@@VERSION`, the UTC-offset `DATEDIFF`, the `DB_ID('rdsadmin')` RDS test, and `HAS_DBACCESS(N'msdb')` are all scalars needing no permission - sets edition/version/offset/rds/msdb and must succeed for any connected login. A best-effort START-TIME query (`SELECT sqlserver_start_time FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info`, the one column that genuinely needs the DMV) runs in its OWN try/catch, so a permission failure leaves ServerStartTime unset without flipping IsOnline or failing detection. The Full Dashboard (which rejects Azure SQL DB as unsupported) now returns the clear "use the Lite edition for Azure SQL DB monitoring" message even for a permission-starved login - previously the coupled probe threw on `sys.dm_os_sys_info` and the outer catch marked the server offline with a raw VIEW-DATABASE-STATE permission error; Darling drops the unused start-time column entirely (its runtime never surfaced a start time, so it needs no second read). Each app's detection SQL is now a pinned const with a regression-guard test asserting it carries no `dm_os_sys_info` and still carries `SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')`. The FinOps inventory display queries (`DatabaseService.FinOps.Inventory` / `LocalDataService.FinOps.ServerProperties`) are a separate concern and untouched. Verified: the full solution builds clean (0 errors); Lite.Tests 1431 passed / 0 failed, Darling.Tests 2734 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped, and the new Dashboard.Tests detection-query class 3 passed / 0 failed.

- **Darling service: file logging now fails LOUDLY instead of going silent** ([#1590]) - #1581 field finding: on the field box the log directory (`%ProgramData%\PerformanceMonitorDarling\logs`) turned unwritable (an ACL artifact) and `DarlingFileLoggerProvider` went SILENT - the 5s flush timer's `catch{}` swallowed every write and `_enabled` was only ever set from the constructor, so nobody learned file logging had died until they went to tail the log. The service was otherwise healthy (Event Log + collectors fine), which is exactly why the dead log went unnoticed. **Fix:** a file-logging FAILURE - the constructor cannot create/enable the directory, OR a flush write throws - now surfaces ONCE through the always-available fallback channel: a best-effort Windows Event Log Warning under the source Program.cs registers ("PerformanceMonitor Darling"). An Interlocked latch bounds it to a SINGLE event for the provider's lifetime (a dir broken on every 5s flush does not spam one warning per flush), the Event Log write is Windows-guarded and fully swallowed (a missing source on a by-the-book `NT SERVICE` install, or a non-Windows host, can never turn logging-about-logging into a crash), and every other discipline (buffered writes, never-throw, retention) is unchanged. The ACL hardening itself is untouched (a separate security decision). Darling service only, no store schema change. Verified: full `Darling.Tests` green (**2741 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped**), including new pins that drive the once-only latch through an injected sink - a healthy provider never surfaces, a constructor directory-failure surfaces exactly once, and repeated failing flushes surface at most once.

- **Darling scheduler: a service restart no longer herds all servers' first catch-up sweep into one tick** ([#1590]) - #1581 field finding: right after a restart, ALL 24 monitored servers fired `"collection body has not completed after 60s - skipping relaunch"` in a burst (366 events over ~10 min, declining) - nothing actually broke (0 collector errors, data landed), but every server's initial catch-up body launched in the SAME sweep tick. Root cause: the fire-and-track launch loop stamps `SweepStartedUtc` at LAUNCH and only N=4 bodies open a connection at once, so a restart launched all 24 first bodies together, ~20 queued behind the gate, and each that waited past 60s logged the (queue-time-inclusive) stall warning - a thundering herd on the heavyweight CONNECT body (which runs XE-session DDL + all on-load collectors). The per-collector [#1577]/#1575 seed jitter did NOT cover this: it staggers WHICH collectors are due once a body runs, not WHEN the connect body itself launches. **Fix:** each initial server's FIRST post-startup body is now held until a deterministic per-server offset elapses - `ColdStartFirstSweepDue` = a captured startup instant + `CadencePhaseOffset(server_id, 150)`, reusing the existing anti-lockstep idiom ([#1553]) capped at the 150s analysis-phase window, NOT a new knob - so 24 servers spread their first launches across ~150s instead of one 15s tick. It gates ONLY the first launch (`InFlightSweep` still null); steady-state cadence, the relaunch/stall path, and the DarlingWorker field-hardening invariants (fire-and-track Retired flag, outer-thread-only sweep state, never-dispose gate, never await-all) are untouched, and a reconcile-ADDED server launches promptly (a single add is not a herd). Darling service only, no store schema change. Verified: full `Darling.Tests` green (**2741 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped**), including a pure decision-table for the offset (deterministic/restart-stable, bounded within [0, 150s), spread across >=10 distinct FNV ids - not all zero, not all equal) and a drift tripwire on the 150s window.

- **Darling service: the compression-job self-heal can now actually re-arm a stuck job (`alter_job` job_id type fix)** ([#1586]) - a correctness follow-up to the [#1585] self-heal (both still unreleased). TimescaleDB's `alter_job` takes `job_id integer`, but the id was carried as a `long` and bound, so Npgsql sent `bigint` and Postgres could not resolve the function (`42883: function alter_job(bigint, ...) does not exist`) - the re-arm always threw. Net effect of the #1585 code as merged: the self-heal correctly DETECTED a stuck compression job and alerted, but every re-arm failed and escalated with a misleading "auto-re-arm FAILED / login does not own the job" message, so nothing was actually healed. **Fix:** cast the bound id `$1::integer` at every `alter_job` call site (a TimescaleDB job_id never exceeds int4, so the cast is always safe). The unit tests missed it because they use a fake re-arm delegate; only the gated-live test exercises the real `alter_job`, and PR CI does not run the gated-live suite - the dev nightly's `Darling PostgreSQL tests` job (real TimescaleDB) caught it. Verified: full `Darling.Tests` green locally (**2734 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped**); the gated-live test proves the live re-arm succeeds against real TimescaleDB (`TryRearmJobAsync` -> `alter_job($1::integer, next_start => now())`) plus the detection query's validity against the real views. The `-infinity`/Running-past-bound detection logic stays in the pure `IsCompressionJobStuck` unit tests because TimescaleDB rejects `alter_job(..., next_start => '-infinity')` with `22023: cannot set next start to -infinity` — the dead-scheduler state arises inside TimescaleDB's own bgw scheduler, not from a user call, so it cannot be injected through the public API (the test's earlier attempt to force it is corrected in [#1588]).

- **Darling service: TimescaleDB compression-job self-heal + single-instance / argument-handling hardening** ([#1585]) - the two durable product fixes from the #1581 field incident, where a prod box climbed toward OOM over ~30h with Windows error 487 ("could not reserve shared memory region"). Root cause, found from box data: three TimescaleDB compression-policy jobs had silently died (`next_start = -infinity`, so the scheduler never re-fired them) on the largest hypertables; compression halted, uncompressed data grew without bound, and autovacuum churned the swelling TOAST until the disk bled and worker-spawns hit the 487 wall. A separate self-inflicted event during triage - a stray `Service.exe --version` that fell through into a real startup and spawned a SECOND service instance fighting the first over the bundled PostgreSQL shared-memory segment - caused a ~7.5h outage. **(A) Stuck-compression-job self-heal:** the worker now runs an hourly, TimescaleDB-only, fleet-level check that reads every compression-policy job from `timescaledb_information.job_stats` JOIN `jobs` and flags one as STUCK when `next_start = -infinity` (compared in SQL, so it never depends on Npgsql's infinity conversion) OR it has been `Running` past `max(2x the schedule interval, a 2h floor)`. A stuck job is re-armed ONCE via a parameterized `alter_job($1, next_start => now())` and a CRITICAL alert fires through the same deliverer/history/cooldown the store-disk-pressure self-alert uses (real email/webhook plus an audit row); if it re-hangs after the self-heal, or the re-arm itself fails (e.g. the store login does not own the job), the service ESCALATES and stops re-arming, and writes a resolution row on recovery. Failure-isolated at three layers so a store hiccup can never abort the collection sweep; a clean no-op on plain-PostgreSQL stores. **(B) Single-instance + argument robustness:** the exe classifies its first argument before building the host - `--version`/`--help` print and exit 0, and any UNKNOWN option prints usage to stderr and exits non-zero WITHOUT starting the host (the `--version` that triggered the outage now prints and exits); recognized one-shot verbs and the no-arg SCM start are unchanged. A `Global\` named single-instance mutex is then acquired between host build and run: a second invocation that reaches startup logs a CRITICAL and exits without starting, so it can never fight the first over PostgreSQL or the MCP/web ports. An abandoned mutex (a previously-crashed instance) is acquired with a warning; the owner releases it on graceful shutdown so an SCM stop-then-start re-acquires cleanly. Windows-gated. Darling service only, no store schema change. Verified: Release build clean, full `Darling.Tests` green (**2723 passed / 0 failed / 142 gated-live skipped**), including pure predicate and state-machine coverage (detection branches; re-arm-once, escalate, re-hang, recovery; both throwing seams isolated), the argument-classification decision table, and the mutex behaviors (fresh, held, abandoned, SCM restart) - plus a gated-live test that forces `next_start = -infinity` on a real compression job to exercise detection and re-arm (the re-arm's `alter_job` job_id type mismatch surfaced there and is fixed in [#1586]).

- **Darling scheduler: a service restart no longer starves long-frequency collectors - NextDue is seeded from the persisted collection watermark, not a full-interval offset** ([#1577]) - a daily collector (`index_object_stats`, CollectorScheduleDefaults frequency 1440 min) read as FAILING across most of the fleet even though every run it *did* complete was a SUCCESS; field-confirmed on DARLING01, where after a day with ~4 deploy restarts it had not run on 4 of 5 servers in ~27h. Root cause: on EVERY connect, `TryConnectAsync` seeded each collector's next-due as `now + CadencePhaseOffset(serverId, frequencySeconds)` (and `RecomputeNextDue`'s new-entry branch did the same) - a deterministic per-server phase (`(uint)serverId % periodSeconds`) that for a daily collector is *anywhere up to ~24h*. The persisted last-collection watermark was never consulted for scheduling, so each restart re-phased every collector up to a full interval forward, discarding when it actually last ran. Minute collectors were invisible to this (their offset is < 60s); long-frequency collectors were **starved indefinitely in a restart-heavy window** - each restart pushed the next run past the following restart.
