The full-interval offset (introduced as [#1553]'s anti-lockstep jitter) is simply too coarse for long cadences, and it also meant a FRESH install waited up to 24h for a daily collector's first run. **Fix:** the seed sites now read each collector's `MAX(collection_time)` in ONE batched round-trip per server and decide the next-due via a new pure `ComputeSeededNextDue(lastRunUtc, frequencyMinutes, nowUtc, jitter)`: a recently-run collector waits out the REMAINING interval (`lastRun + interval`), an overdue one runs promptly, and a never-run one runs shortly after connect - overdue/never-run stamped at `now + a small per-server jitter` capped at `min(interval, 150s)` (mirroring the analysis-phase jitter) to de-cluster the fleet WITHOUT the full-interval defer. A restart now RESUMES the real cadence instead of re-phasing it, a daily collector actually collects daily across restarts, and a fresh install runs it minutes after first connect (then daily). The watermark read is failure-isolated (a store hiccup falls back to a prompt jittered run, never aborting the connect), the new-entry branch of the reload recompute gets the same treatment (lazily, at most one extra round-trip per server that gains a collector), and the steady-state advance in `RunDueCollectorsAsync` (already the exact interval) is unchanged. Distinct from #1573, which fixes the *banding* (a daily collector shouldn't read FAILING at 25h); both are needed - with only #1573 the collector still wouldn't run, with only this the banding would still mis-flag the legitimate gap. Darling service only, no store schema change; the DarlingWorker field-hardening invariants (fire-and-track Retired flag, outer-thread-only sweep state, never-dispose gate, never await-all) are untouched. Verified: the Darling service builds clean in Release and the full `Darling.Tests` suite is green (**2403 passed / 0 failed / 138 gated-live skipped**), including a pure decision-table for the seed policy (daily recently-run waits ~23h; daily overdue runs promptly; 1-min recently-run keeps its ~30s feel; daily never-run seeds within ~150s - the fresh-install fix; the overdue boundary is inclusive) and the jitter cap (deterministic per FNV id, capped at `min(interval, 150s)`), plus a gated-live pin that the batched read returns `MAX(collection_time)` per collector relabeled Kind=Utc.
- **Collector health: a healthy DAILY collector no longer bands STALE/FAILING, and no longer inflates the Overview "collectors failing" count** ([#1578]) - the per-collector health status used FLAT thresholds (STALE past 4h since last success, FAILING past 24h) that assumed a frequent (~1-min) collector. `index_object_stats` is a DAILY collector (`CollectorScheduleDefaults` cadence 1440 min) that succeeds every run, so between its once-a-day successes it always crossed 4h and then 24h and read STALE then FAILING - a real field false-positive. Because the Overview card's "Collectors: N failing" count reuses the SAME row banding (it counts `HealthStatus == "FAILING"`), a healthy daily collector also showed the fleet Overview a phantom failing collector. The staleness thresholds are now RELATIVE to each collector's own cadence: **FAILING when hours-since-success > max(24, 2 x freqHours)** and **STALE when > max(4, 1.5 x freqHours)**. The floors are the original flat values, so every FREQUENT collector bands byte-for-byte identically (a 1-min collector still goes stale at 4h and fails at 24h; an hourly collector too, since 1.5h/2h are under the floors); only a slow collector relaxes - a daily collector now goes stale at 36h and fails at 48h, so `index_object_stats` at 27h reads HEALTHY. On-load config snapshots (`server_config` / `database_config` / `database_scoped_config` / `trace_flags` / `server_properties`) stay staleness-exempt (banded by failure rate only), and NEVER_RUN / NO_PERMISSIONS / WARNING are unchanged. The banding was **three byte-identical copies** that nothing pinned together (the #1573 core) - Lite's grid, the Darling viewer's grid + Overview count, and the service's `get_collection_health` MCP tool + web fleet failing-count reader; all three now delegate to ONE pure classifier, `CollectorHealthClassifier` in `PerformanceMonitor.Common` (alongside the #1562 per-server card classifier), which also centralizes the on-load set so the surfaces cannot drift. Each surface resolves the collector's cadence from the shared `CollectorScheduleDefaults` default (no consumer cheaply has a per-install schedule override at the row level, so all three use the same default - which is itself the parity guarantee); an unknown collector name falls to the 24h/4h floors, i.e. the old flat behavior. A mirrored decision-table test in Lite.Tests and Darling.Tests pins the whole table - including that `index_object_stats`' default 1440-min cadence yields HEALTHY at 27h, the exact bug - so the two SKUs can never drift.
- **Darling: the retention purge's batched DELETE no longer breaks on compressed chunks** ([#1567]) - the purge's row-capped `ctid IN (SELECT ctid ... LIMIT 10000)` batching idiom cannot execute against a TimescaleDB table with a compressed chunk in range: reading the `ctid` system column through transparent decompression is unsupported ("transparent decompression only supports tableoid system column", reproduced on the pinned 2.28.1), so the whole statement errored, the per-table isolation swallowed it as a warning, and the table silently kept its expired rows. In production that broke the DELETE fallback exactly when it matters - on a hypertable with compressed chunks whose `drop_chunks` transiently failed - and on plain-PostgreSQL stores pointed at a Timescale-compressed table. The batcher now deletes the OLDEST one-day slice of expired rows per statement (a plain time-range predicate rides TimescaleDB's supported DML decompression; the per-DML decompression cap is lifted on the purge connection, a placeholder-safe SET on stores without the extension). A day is one chunk on a hypertable and one day's arrival volume on a plain table - the same bounded-work goal the row cap served. Found via #1564: the gated purge tests flaked order-dependently because the shared fixture's compression policy jobs run immediately on creation and compressed the test chunks mid-suite; those tests now assert only their own-scoped contract (global activity counts stay order-dependent), carry a capturing logger so any purge warning lands in the failure text instead of a null logger, and a new deterministic E2E compresses a chunk synchronously and proves the DELETE path clears expired rows inside it.

- **Darling: the viewer's store pool is now capped too, and the postgres.exe process count is documented for what it actually is** ([#1566]) â€” round-4 field verification correctly flagged that the [#1559] "â‰¤24 backends" claim didn't hold: the process count oscillated to 44 during checkpoint/catch-up waves. Two truths behind that: **(1)** the peaks are overwhelmingly **TimescaleDB background policy workers**, not client connections â€” the managed conf deliberately sizes `timescaledb.max_background_workers` to hypertables + 2 (â‰ˆ38), and every running compression/retention job is its own postgres.exe, so the count legitimately surges during policy waves and recedes (the field box's OS free memory was rock-stable through every swing); **(2)** the claim was also genuinely incomplete â€” the co-located **viewer built its connection string independently and rode Npgsql's default pool of 100**; it is now capped at 10 (a read-only UI seat on 30/60-second timers). The README's troubleshooting section gains the process-anatomy note with the `pg_stat_activity GROUP BY backend_type` decomposition query and the reminder that Windows charges the shared buffer segment to every attached process's working set, so per-process memory numbers cannot be summed.

- **query_store collector: the self-exclusion filter was 75% of the read's cost â€” bounded to a 100-char scan; per-database log lines; rdsadmin default-excluded** ([#1565]) â€” a field actual plan (103k-row burst, 14.9s elapsed) put 11.2s in ONE Filter node: `query_sql_text NOT LIKE N'%PerformanceMonitorLite%'`, a per-row substring scan over full nvarchar(max) query text that excludes only ~2% of rows (A/B: 15.0s with, 3.5s without â€” 4.3Ã—). No predicate shape fixes that server-side â€” Query Store's internal text table has no index on the column, so every variant is a residual scan priced in bytes read. The predicate is therefore **removed from the SQL entirely** (the query now runs the exact no-filter form the field A/B measured) and the exclusion happens **client-side in `ReadItemAsync`**, where the query text is already materialized for every row: a marker `Contains` drops self rows before they enter the batch â€” never stored, never counted against the byte budget, identical semantics, zero SQL cost. Three companions from the same report: **(1) per-database log lines** â€” the per-server summary blended every database into one number, hiding a single database's 50-second burst behind quiet siblings; the enumeration driver's completion hook now carries per-item SQL/storage timings and both apps log `  [server] query_store [Database] => N rows (sql:â€¦ms, â€¦)` for non-empty batches (quiet databases stay silent), with the per-server roll-up unchanged; **(2) a canonical default screen** on the Query Store enumeration (both shared paths + the Dashboard proc): vendor management databases (`rdsadmin`, `gcloud_cloudsqladmin`), SSRS catalogs (`ReportServer`/`ReportServerTempDB`), PolyBase/DW artifacts (`DWConfiguration`/`DWDiagnostics`/`DWQueue`), the system four as a name-based belt, and â€” by operator decision â€” the DBA-convention tooling names `DBAUtil`/`DBAUtils`/`Utility` (a shop whose real workload database uses one of those names should rename or accept the gap; `excludedDatabases` handles the inverse case); **(3) the ~15-minute burst cadence documented** in the collector and the README troubleshooting section â€” Query Store buffers in memory and flushes on `DATA_FLUSH_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (default 900s), so two-of-three near-empty cycles followed by a burst is the source's behavior, not a watermark bug, and narrowing the poll interval will not smooth it. Field-verified alongside: the [#1557] byte budget held its first real soak (61 clean minutes; the database that previously OOM'd the host hit the budget gracefully instead).

- **Darling: the viewer's MCP toggle now actually starts and stops the MCP server â€” live, no service restart** ([#1560]) â€” the Settings checkbox has always written the control plane (`config_service.mcp_enabled` / `mcp_port`), and the worker's reload beacon has always applied those values to its live config â€” but `DarlingMcpHostService` read **only `darling.json`, once, at startup**, then either bound forever or stood down forever. So the viewer toggle changed a value nothing consulted (field-confirmed: no file line appears, and restarting doesn't help, because the restart re-reads the same unchanged file). The host is now a **supervisor**: the worker publishes the live enabled/port values through a shared in-process seam (`McpRuntimeState`, one immutable snapshot â€” never a torn pair) at startup and on every reload, and the host polls it every 5 seconds â€” starting the inner web app when the toggle flips on, stopping it (and, in managed network mode, reconciling the firewall rule off) when it flips off, and rebinding on a port change. A failed start attempt (port in use, store credential not ready yet) now retries on a 30-second backoff instead of standing down for the process lifetime. Deliberate boundary: the `mcp.network` LAN-exposure block (bind IP, CIDR, bearer token) stays file-defined and restart-only â€” changing an exposure surface should require touching the host â€” but the store toggle stops an exposed endpoint instantly, which makes it a clean kill switch. The supervisor's decision table, the state seam's coherence, and the 5s/30s cadences are unit-pinned; `darling.sample.json` and the README now describe the file values as the first-run seed with the live toggle in Settings.

- **Darling managed store: size shared_buffers for the co-located reality, healing the Windows backend-spawn failures** ([#1559]) â€” the v3 memory sizing derived `shared_buffers` as min(25% RAM, 8 GB), the PostgreSQL docs' starting point â€” but the docs condition that figure on "a dedicated database server," and the managed store is deliberately the opposite: co-located with the service, the viewer, and often Lite, double-caching against a shared OS cache. On Windows the oversize segment has a sharper cost: every backend process must re-reserve the shared memory region at the postmaster's base address, and the pgsql-bugs history (BUG #14050/#18954) documents larger `shared_buffers` exacerbating `could not reserve shared memory region ... error code 487` spawn failures under ASLR/DLL address pressure â€” observed live on the 16 GB field box (4 GB segment, recurring 487s, checkpointer working set â‰ˆ the whole buffer pool). The derivation is now **min(25% RAM, 1 GB)**; existing clusters heal via a new v5 conf block that re-states the capped value (postgresql.conf takes the last occurrence, so the override wins without rewriting v3), and the service's store connection strings gain `MaxPoolSize = 24` so the backend-process count â€” 43 observed during a 24-server sweep, each a spawn through the 487 surface â€” is bounded at the client too. Also worth knowing when reading Task Manager: Windows charges shared_buffers pages to EVERY backend's working set, so N processes "totaling" gigabytes are mostly the same shared segment counted N times, and a ~4.5-minute checkpoint `write=` phase is deliberate checkpoint spreading, not distress â€” two signals the field investigation initially (and reasonably) misread.

- **Query Store: stop collecting from readable-secondary replicas â€” their Query Store is the primary's replicated content, not local activity** ([#1558]) â€” a field fleet of 24 RDS for SQL Server read replicas was collecting query_store from every replica: each database's `sys.database_query_store_options` showed `actual_state = READ_ONLY`, `desired_state = READ_WRITE`, `readonly_reason = 8` â€” the engine's documented "read-only BECAUSE this database is a readable secondary replica." A secondary's persisted Query Store tables are the PRIMARY's content arriving via replication, so collecting there yields duplicate, lagged primary data â€” 24 copies of the same Query Store, which is also exactly the kind of pathological volume that fed the [#1557] memory incident. The existing replica guard only caught **Always On AG** secondaries (the enumeration's `sys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states` join) â€” an RDS read replica is not an AG member, so it passed the "not in any AG" arm looking like a standalone primary, and the [#1546] eligibility gate (`actual_state IN (1, 2, 4)`) deliberately admits READ_ONLY without consulting *why* it is read-only. The per-database eligibility check now adds `readonly_reason & 8 = 0` (bitmask form) at all three sites â€” the shared collector's on-prem and Azure enumeration queries (Lite + Darling) and the deprecated Dashboard's `install/09` proc â€” using the engine's own mechanism-agnostic replica flag, so AG secondaries, RDS read replicas, and geo-secondaries are all skipped uniformly. Deliberately still collected: an operator-set read-only Query Store on a primary (`readonly_reason <> 8` â€” stale-but-real local data, the [#1546] decision), and a 2022+/2025 `READ_CAPTURE_SECONDARY` (state 4), where a secondary captures its own genuinely-local workload. Collect Query Store from the primary; the replicas were never telling you anything it wasn't.

- **Collectors: a stalled query_store cycle can no longer run the collector out of memory and take the box down â€” reads are byte-bounded, writes flush per database, and an over-memory service stops launching new work** ([#1557]) â€” responds to a field incident: a 24-server Darling fleet's memory climbed from ~0 to ~13GB in nine minutes, exhausted the Windows commit limit, and took down the bundled PostgreSQL and the host with it. The culprit was `query_store`. Three mechanisms compounded. The per-database Query Store read is UNBOUNDED and carries the full plan XML on every runtime-stats interval row, so a busy or pathological database streams gigabytes. The runner then accumulated EVERY database's rows into one `List<TRow>` before a single write, so peak memory was the whole fleet's rows at once. And the per-item `catch` swallowed `OutOfMemoryException` and kept going with that list retained, so the process kept trying rather than failing fast â€” while [#1553]'s 4-wide sweep multiplied the peak Ã—4. No single cap fixes this (a row cap bounds COUNT, not BYTES; a per-database flush bounds ACROSS databases, not WITHIN one), so the fix is a **composed** bound whose guaranteed transient peak is **â‰ˆ 4 Ã— 256 MB â‰ˆ 1 GB** â€” bounded on a 16 GB host. **(1) Per-database byte budget (the PRIMARY bound)** â€” `QueryStoreCollector.ReadItemAsync` now accumulates the two `nvarchar(max)` fields' materialized size (char count Ã— 2 for UTF-16) as it reads and STOPS at `MaxTextBytesPerDatabase = 256 MB`, disposing the reader early and signalling truncation (surfaced as a WARNING naming the server and database). This is what actually bounds bytes: 50k rows each carrying a ~40KB plan is ~2GB for ONE database, and a row cap fires no defense against it. **(2) Per-database flush** â€” both runners (Darlingâ†’Postgres, Liteâ†’DuckDB), on BOTH multi-item paths (the enumeration collectors and the Azure per-database path), now write each database's rows before reading the next, through a new shared `EnumeratedCollectorDriver` (a delegate-driven control-flow loop, extracted so the byte-identical duplication that let this bug live in two runners is gone) and a single per-app `WriteBatchAsync` â€” one pooled store connection per body, one binary COPY / appender per database, so a mid-run abort keeps batches 1..N-1 committed. Peak memory is now ONE database's bounded rows, not the fleet's. **(3) Server-side backstops** â€” the shared per-item query gains `TOP (50000)` + `ORDER BY last_execution_time DESC` (a coarse ceiling bounding SQL work and wire transfer before the client budget engages), and the plan-capturing (Darling) path dedupes plan XML to ONE runtime-stats interval per `plan_id` per cycle via `ROW_NUMBER()` (NULL on the rest) â€” the Query Store readers already tolerate a per-row-NULL plan (Lite fetches plans live; the Darling stored-plan readers guard `query_plan_text IS NOT NULL`; the actual-plan resolver's `ORDER BY` now leads with `(query_plan_text IS NOT NULL) DESC` so it prefers the plan-bearing interval). **(4) Per-database watermark for query_store** (`PerDatabaseWatermarkColumn => "database_name"`, the deadlocks/BPR precedent) â€” because the collector now commits per database, its cutoff must be per database, or a mid-run abort would strand the un-flushed databases' intervals behind the committed databases' advanced server-wide watermark; each database's commit now advances only its own watermark and an abort loses nothing. **(5) 24h catch-up clamp** â€” a new `WatermarkPolicy.ClampCatchup` floors a >24h-stale watermark to `now âˆ’ 24h`, applied at EXACTLY the query_store per-database cutoff computation inside the enumeration loop (the site that now exists because of change 4): a service down for days must not issue a cutoff pointing days into the retained-30-day source and pull the whole backlog in one cycle. Deliberately scoped to query_store ONLY â€” for a ring-buffer / rolling-trace source (`default_trace` on a quiet server spans days) the clamp would wrongly truncate legitimate catch-up, so the generic watermark reads are untouched; a routine restart never clamps, a multi-day outage survives with a bounded, logged history hole. **(6) Working-set launch guard (Darling)** â€” before launching any new collection bodies each tick, the fleet sweep checks `Process.PrivateMemorySize64` (the committed-private metric that matched the incident, not the GC heap) against `0.80 Ã—` `GC.GetGCMemoryInfo().TotalAvailableMemoryBytes`; over the line it launches NOTHING that tick so in-flight bodies drain (purge / disk-pressure / delay keep running), logging ONE fleet-level CRITICAL per episode and an Information line on recovery. **(7) OOM semantics** â€” the per-item swallow-catch is filtered `not OutOfMemoryException` and OOM propagates (there is no cross-database accumulator left to clear â€” the per-database batch is a local that unwinds), so the host classifies the run ERROR rather than spinning; that ERROR handler's own `collection_log` write is wrapped best-effort so a secondary allocation failure can't fault the fire-and-track body. Also fixes a **latent** bug found in passing: Darling's Azure per-database read passed the constant 60s timeout where Lite honored `CommandTimeoutSecondsOverride`, so `index_object_stats` (which needs 300s per database on Azure) would have timed out at 60s â€” Darling now honors the override, pinned. **Contracts**: `collection_log` totals are unchanged (row counts and wall-clock are preserved; the SQL/storage split is now a sum of interleaved slices); the ONLY watermark-granularity change is query_store gaining a per-database watermark; the 24h clamp is a deliberate, logged catch-up bound (the source retains the older data; the viewer's windows are 24h); the deprecated Full Dashboard receives ONLY the `TOP`/`ORDER BY` mirror in `install/09_collect_query_store.sql` (NOT the dedupe â€” its "Download Plan" reads a plan by exact `collection_id`, where per-row NULLs would break a real reader). The two watermark-less enumeration snapshot collectors (`database_scoped_config`, `index_object_stats`) were verified to tolerate the per-database partial commits a mid-run abort can leave: their latest-snapshot readers key on `MAX(capture_time)` or dedupe per index identity, and a per-database SKIP already produced that same "partial at the latest timestamp" shape today, so no new failure mode is introduced. Verified: the Darling service, Darling.Tests, Lite, and Lite.Tests all build clean in Release; the full suites are green â€” **Lite.Tests 1369 passed / 0 failed / 0 skipped**, **Darling.Tests 2161 passed / 0 failed / 134 gated-live skipped** â€” including new pins for the shared driver's control flow (per-item flush, no cross-item accumulation, empty-batch skip, per-item skip, OOM rethrow, cancellation propagation), the `WatermarkPolicy` clamp boundaries, the `ShouldLaunchSweeps` bands + the 0.80 tripwire, the query_store `TOP`/`ORDER BY`/dedupe SQL + per-database bounds properties, and the Azure timeout-override resolution. No store schema change.
- **Darling: one slow server can no longer strand the rest of the fleet â€” the collection sweep goes bounded-parallel with cadence jitter and per-server exception containment** ([#1553]) â€” tier 2 of the 24-server field-incident response (tier 1 was [#1552]'s diagnostic surfaces). The service swept the fleet STRICTLY SEQUENTIALLY: one `foreach` over every server, with every step â€” connect, XE ensure, the full on-load collector suite, alert eval, analysis â€” awaited inline before the next server was touched. Under store distress a single server's first-connect work took 3+ minutes, so the alphabetical tail went unreached for tens of minutes, indistinguishable from being permanently dropped (the exact field report), and every 15-second cadence boundary re-herded all servers at once. Three coordinated changes. **(1) Fire-and-track sweep** â€” the loop now LAUNCHES each server's collection body without awaiting it (a new `ProcessServerSweepAsync`, tracked per-server in `InFlightSweep`) and falls straight through to the fleet-level steps, so one slow or hung server can no longer head-of-line-block the other 23 or the reload / purge / disk-check steps. Concurrency is bounded to **N=4** (`MaxConcurrentServerSweeps`, hardcoded â€” defaults-over-config, no knob) by a shared `SemaphoreSlim` acquired INSIDE each body, so at most four SQL connections open at once while collectors stay sequential within a server (Lite's `RemoteCollectorService` shape â€” parallel across servers, sequential collectors per server); a 24-server worst case clears in ~6 waves. Deliberately NOT await-all-per-sweep: awaiting the whole wave recreates the incident in miniature â€” one server hung 3 min stalls the NEXT sweep for all 23 healthy servers â€” so decoupling, the actual tier-2 goal, wins. **(2) Cadence jitter** â€” a deterministic, restart-stable per-server phase offset (`CadencePhaseOffset` = `(uint)serverId % periodSeconds` seconds; `serverId` is already an FNV-1a hash so no further mixing is needed, and there is NO `Random`, so a restart re-derives the SAME phases instead of re-herding) is applied ONLY at initial cadence stamps â€” each collector's first post-connect due time, a reload's newly-enabled collectors, and the first scheduled analysis (phased over a fixed 2.5-minute window, NOT the full analysis interval, so an already-populated store still analyzes promptly). The steady-state advance is never jittered, and the light 30-second alert / self-alert cadence is deliberately left un-phased (it is an order lighter than a collector open and its unreachable-server edge is already edge-fired, not sweep-gated). **(3) Per-server exception containment** â€” the extracted body is wrapped in a catch-all (the gap the sweep loop previously acknowledged it had no cover for: with no per-server catch, one server's unexpected throw could kill collection for the whole fleet), so a throw is logged and isolated and a faulted fire-and-track task can never surface unobserved (`ThrowUnobservedTaskExceptions` is not set, confirmed). Because the sweep is now concurrent, three supporting invariants are enforced. Per-server `NextDue` becomes a `ConcurrentDictionary` â€” a reload's recompute on the outer thread can now touch it while a body reads and advances it; it is only ever INDEXED by the static collector-catalog keys (never enumerated), so this is a drop-in that eliminates the one structure the old strict single-threaded invariant existed to protect from tearing. Disabling a server marks its state **Retired** â€” checked as the first statement inside each body immediately after it acquires the gate (an async method runs synchronously only to its first await, so a check before the queue-able wait would evaluate at launch time and a gate-queued body would never re-check on dequeue), plus a second re-check on the connect path before any durable side-effect â€” so an in-flight or gate-queued body for a just-removed server no-ops: it never connects, never runs XE `CREATE SESSION` DDL against a disabled target, and never re-writes self-alert edge state after the reconcile's `Forget`. And shutdown DRAINS the in-flight bodies (up to 15 seconds, budgeted inside the host's default 30-second `ShutdownTimeout` with headroom for the serial command-loop drain) before awaiting the command loop; the fleet gate is deliberately never disposed, so a body still finishing past the drain â€” or a removed server's detached body â€” cannot hit `Release()` on a disposed semaphore. Because the field incident was HANGS, not throws â€” which the catch-all does not cover â€” the skip branch is instrumented (all its state outer-thread-owned): a body still running when its server comes up for relaunch logs Debug each sweep and ONE Warning per episode once it crosses 60 seconds CONTINUOUS (queue time counts â€” a body stuck behind the N=4 gate is genuinely unserved â€” and the text does not claim slot occupancy, since a queued body holds no slot), with an Info recording the total elapsed when it finally completes. **Contracts unchanged**: `collection_log` write semantics, the MCP surfaces, and alert dedup / edge semantics are all preserved; the per-server CollectionGate's snapshot_now-waits / scheduled-sweep-skips behavior is unchanged (bodies just moved to pool threads); and disabling a server now provably incurs zero side-effects via the Retired flag. Darling service only â€” no store schema change, no Lite / Dashboard / viewer changes. Verified: the Darling service, Darling.Tests, and Lite all build clean; the full Darling.Tests (2157 passed / 134 gated-live skipped) and Lite.Tests (1352 passed) suites are green, including six new `DarlingSweepSchedulingTests` pinning the jitter offset (deterministic, unsigned-modulo including negative FNV ids, bounded [0, period), not-all-identical across â‰¥10 realistic ids, non-positive period â†’ zero) and the N=4 concurrency cap.
- **Index Analysis: the monitor-side dedupe engine absorbs the sp_IndexCleanup review fixes it predated** ([#1548]) â€” the shared `IndexCleanupAnalyzer` (the Darling FinOps Index Analysis engine â€” Lite runs the real proc live and is unaffected) was ported from `sp_IndexCleanup` on July 5; the proc's adversarial review then fixed sixteen defects across July 14â€“16 (`ae32a4c`, `0abb3ef`), and the port still carried every analysis-relevant one. Fixed to review parity: **(1) phantom partitioning key** â€” the shared `IndexObjectStatsCollector` key aggregation filtered only `is_included_column = 0`, so on a partitioned index the partitioning column (riding at `key_ordinal = 0` when it is not a real key) landed FIRST in the captured `key_columns`, poisoning every duplicate/subset/constraint comparison; the aggregation now requires `key_ordinal > 0` (stored rows self-heal on the next collection cycle). **(2) Constraint-backed merge targets** â€” a PK-backed index could be picked as a Rule-5 keeper (the old keeper sort put `Protected` FIRST) or as a Rules-3/4/6 superset target, drawing a `CREATE INDEX ... DROP_EXISTING` rebuild that fails outright (Msg 1907) while the losers' DISABLEs succeeded â€” coverage destroyed with nothing absorbing it; PK/UC-backed indexes are now excluded from both picks (new `IsSafeMergeTarget`), with a script-generator backstop that emits a no-script REVIEW row if one ever slips through. **(3) Filtered / partitioned / non-nonclustered MAKE UNIQUE promotion** â€” Rule 7's marking had no filter guard (a filtered unique index enforces uniqueness only over its predicate: replacing a constraint with one opens a uniqueness hole), and Rule 7.5 step 2 skipped only unique constraints, so a clustered or PK-backed exact-key row could be promoted; a single `IsPromotableToUnique` gate (nonclustered, non-constraint-backed, unfiltered, unpartitioned â€” the partitioning column isn't captured, so the Msg-1908 alignment test is impossible monitor-side and partitioned candidates are conservatively skipped) now guards Rule 7, and CRITICALLY the same gate guards Rule 7.5 **step 1's replacement pick**, so a constraint is never marked DROP CONSTRAINT pointing at an index step 2 refuses to promote (an orphaned constraint drop = uniqueness silently gone). **(4) Inert usage floors** â€” the `MinReads`/`MinWrites` object gate kept a table if it cleared EITHER floor, but an unset floor's term was `>= 0` (always true), so setting just one floor filtered nothing; each term is now gated on its own threshold being set, mirroring the proc's fixed `HAVING` exactly (latent today â€” both hosts pass 0/0). One deliberate non-port, documented in the analyzer header: the proc's per-INDEX usage floors (`meets_usage_floor`) stay out until a floor knob actually ships â€” both hosts hardwire the floors to 0, so that machinery would be unreachable. Nine new regression tests pin every guard; the 53 existing analyzer tests (including the legitimate merge and MAKE UNIQUE happy paths) pass unchanged.
- **Query Store: attribute runtime stats to their replica role, so an AG's primary stops silently reporting secondary workload as its own** ([#1546]) â€” filed under Fixed rather than Added because the new column is only the mechanism: the DEFECT is that on SQL Server 2022+ with "Query Store for secondary replicas" enabled, every replica shares ONE Query Store that lives on the PRIMARY (secondary workload is streamed to the primary and persisted in the primary's QS tables, distinguishable only by `replica_group_id`), so our Top Queries grids have been silently BLENDING secondary-replica workload into the primary's own CPU/duration/reads numbers with no way for a reader to tell. (Microsoft's own Query Performance Insight has this exact bug.) We collect only from primaries (`is_primary_replica = 1`) â€” correct, and unchanged. The shared `QueryStoreCollector` now LEFT JOINs `sys.query_store_replicas` on `replica_group_id` and selects `replica_role = qsr.replica_name`, gated on `productVersion >= 16`, mirroring the collector's existing version-gated column-fragment pattern; the column is surfaced as a narrow "Replica" column in Lite's and the Darling viewer's Query Store grids, and â€” critically â€” is added as a **GROUP BY key** in the top-queries readers (Lite `LocalDataService.QueryStore`, Darling `ViewerDataService.QueryStore`, and the Darling MCP `DarlingDataReader.QueryStoreTopSql` / `get_query_store_top`), because merely displaying the role while still averaging across replicas would attach a label that actively lies about the numbers beside it. On a standalone/non-AG server every row shares one value, so the added grouping is a no-op and the grids are unchanged. **Three empirical findings drove this shape, each contradicting the Microsoft docs â€” verified live against SQL 2022 (16.0.4255.1) and SQL 2025 (17.0.4045.5); do not "correct" the code back toward the docs:** (1) the docs say `sys.query_store_replicas` is 2025+, but it and `sys.query_store_runtime_stats.replica_group_id` BOTH exist from SQL 2022 â€” hence the `>= 16` gate, not `>= 17`; (2) the docs say `replica_name` is NULL except on Azure Hyperscale named replicas, but it IS populated on box SQL Server (`Primary`, `Secondary`, `Geo Secondary`, `Geo HA Secondary`) â€” hence `replica_name` is read DIRECTLY rather than hand-rolling the docs' sample `CASE`, which additionally maps `replica_group_id` as though it were `role_type` (it is not â€” it is a replica *set* number that accumulates per role across failovers); (3) the join MUST be a LEFT JOIN â€” on a 2022 standalone (non-AG) server `sys.query_store_replicas` has ZERO rows while real runtime-stats rows still carry `replica_group_id = 1`, so an INNER JOIN or a `WHERE replica_name = 'Primary'` filter would match nothing and **silently delete ALL Query Store collection on every 2022 standalone server**. Resulting values: NULL on a 2022 standalone, `'Primary'` on a 2025 standalone, the actual role on an AG with the feature on â€” NULL honestly means "the server did not attribute this row" and is deliberately NOT coalesced to an invented value, so blank is expected and correct. Storage: `replica_role` is APPENDED last in `PayloadColumns` (Lite DuckDB v47, Darling Postgres V28) â€” both hosts' bulk writers are positional (DuckDB appender / Npgsql binary COPY) and an upgraded store gets the column from an `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`, which can only append, so a mid-list position would desync a fresh catalog-generated store from an upgraded one and write shifted values; V28 also re-expands the pinned `v_query_store_stats` `SELECT *`. Same shape as `deadlocks.database_name` ([#1535]). **Also tightens the "is Query Store usable" gate from `actual_state > 0` to `actual_state IN (1, 2, 4)`** (READ_ONLY / READ_WRITE / READ_CAPTURE_SECONDARY) at both `QueryStoreCollector` sites and in `install/09_collect_query_store.sql`: `> 0` also admits `3` = ERROR, letting an *errored* Query Store pass as usable (`0` = OFF). The deprecated Full Dashboard receives ONLY the `actual_state` fix â€” no replica attribution, no schema column, no UI â€” deliberately. NOT changed: the QS regression / comparison / trend / analysis-fact readers still aggregate across replicas; those group at a deliberately coarser grain (query_hash, or a time bucket) and re-grouping them would redefine what a "regression" is and change alert semantics â€” a design decision, not a mechanical mirror. Verified: Lite, Darling viewer, and Darling service all build with 0 errors; the collector definition pins (payload 53â†’54 columns, the new `>= 16` LEFT JOIN gate, and the pre-2022 NULL placeholder), the DuckDB golden-schema equivalence pin, the PgMigrations-vs-`StorageVersion` pin, and both capability pins are green (33 Lite / 63 Darling).
- **Lite: the plan verbs no longer sit on every grid â€” only on the query grids that can actually use them** ([#1545]) â€” Lite had ONE context menu (`DataGridContextMenu`) carrying "View Stored Plan" + "Get Actual Plan (re-run)", attached via `GridRowStyle` to all **27** grids â€” including Server Config, Database Config, Trace Flags, Latch/Spinlock Stats, Plan Cache, the Memory Broker and every other non-query grid, where `ServerTab.Plans.cs`'s row-type switch has no case, so the click silently did nothing. (The Darling viewer never had this â€” its non-query grids are plan-free by construction.) The plain menu is now the plan-less **default** (copy / repro / export) for the 21 non-query grids, and a new `DataGridPlanContextMenu` + `GridPlanRowStyle` carries the plan verbs on the **6** grids whose row types the handlers actually support (Active Queries, Top Queries + its comparison, Top Procedures + its comparison, Query Store). The Query Store *comparison* grid stays plan-less â€” neither handler has a case for its rows. **"Get Actual Plan (re-run)" is additionally hidden per-row** via a new `CanGetActualPlan` flag, because the re-run handler supports fewer row types than View Stored Plan does (the Procedure rows have no re-run case), so it no longer appears where it would no-op â€” the same hide-don't-grey treatment the Darling viewer got in [#1543]. Lite only; capability-pin grid-style + menu-command baselines updated.
- **Darling viewer: stop the hard crash when interacting with the system-tray icon (Hardcodet TrayToolTip issue #422)** ([#1538]) â€” hovering or clicking the tray icon could throw `ArgumentException: The root Visual of a VisualTarget cannot have a parent` from `Popup.CreateWindow` on the custom `TrayToolTip`, unhandled, killing the process (the tray icon just vanished). Lite has suppressed this since `562269f6` / `50257182` via an `IsTrayToolTipCrash` check in BOTH its Dispatcher (`e.Handled = true`) and AppDomain handlers, but the later port of Lite's tray code to the Darling viewer (`f25fe3a0`) copied the crash-prone custom `TrayToolTip` Border AND the comment claiming the handler suppresses the crash WITHOUT porting the actual suppression â€” a parity gap that left the viewer's `App.xaml.cs` handlers logging-and-dying. This ports Lite's exact `IsTrayToolTipCrash` detector + the Dispatcher (`e.Handled`) and AppDomain suppression into the viewer and corrects the stale comment, restoring Liteâ†”Darling parity with the proven handling.
- **Charts: increase the shared top headroom from 5% to 15% so a series that plateaus at a hard ceiling no longer reads as clipped at the top** ([#1538]) â€” `ChartStyle.ComputeYLimitsWithLegendPadding` gave only 5%-of-range breathing room above the data max, so the CPU Scheduler pressure chart's Blocked-Tasks series (pinned at the scheduler count under HammerDB load) sat at ~95% of the plot height and looked cut off. Bumped the top padding to 15% (the data max now lands at ~87%); the pinned Y-limit math tests in `Lite.Tests` + `Dashboard.Tests` are updated. Applies to all charts via the shared `Ui.ChartStyle` (Lite / Darling / the deprecated Dashboard) â€” one consistent, more comfortable headroom, no per-app drift.

- **The gated live-PostgreSQL test suite runs green for the first time â€” one real grant gap and five latent test defects** ([#1551]) â€” the nightly `darling-pg` job had never actually executed: it was added July 2, but scheduled nightly runs execute the workflow file from `main`, which predates the job entirely, so its first real run was a manual dispatch on July 17 â€” which failed wall-to-wall. Fixed, in dependency order: **(1) the throwaway cluster now initdbs its superuser as `darling`** (it used `postgres`, but the V8 schema-split migration runs `CREATE SCHEMA ... AUTHORIZATION darling`, so every fresh-store migration died with `role "darling" does not exist`); with migrations unblocked, the suite exposed **(2) a real viewer-role grant gap** â€” V18 added `alert_delivery_mode_override` to `config_monitored_servers` without adding it to `DarlingManagedRoles`' fail-closed non-secret column list, so a read-only `viewer` seat (including a remote network viewer, whose pg_hba default is `viewer`) was silently denied the per-server delivery-mode override column; the live ratchet test caught it exactly as designed, and the column is now granted. The remaining four were test defects that had never run: **(3)** the two `SeedAndRead ... AgainstScratchPostgres` round-trips assumed an isolated store but ran against the shared CI database, where `SeedIfEmptyAsync` correctly no-ops after any earlier test seeds â€” each now mints (and drops) its own scratch database; **(4)** the system_health planted-events test INSERTed a `collection_id` column that never existed on `system_health_events` (its id column is `system_health_event_id`); **(5)** the managed-bootstrap E2E derived its expected `max_worker_processes` from the catalog count while the product correctly sizes from `TimescaleSupport.HypertableCount` (catalog **plus** `collection_log`); and **(6)** the TimescaleDB E2E asserted `collection_log` becomes a hypertable via the V23 migration alone, but V23 is deliberately a guarded upgrade fast-path that skips when the extension does not exist yet â€” the test now exercises `EnsureCollectionLogHypertableAsync`, the authoritative runtime path the service calls on every start (which is also how a fresh managed store converges). No collector, store, or viewer behavior changes; the only product change is the viewer-role column grant.


- **Darling viewer: query-tab plan menus fixed â€” no greyed-out verbs, right-click acts on the clicked row, and the Regressions / live Active Queries grids get plan access** ([#1543]) â€” several issues surfaced during live testing: (1) the shared plan context menu showed **greyed-out** "View Cached Plan" / "Get Actual Plan (re-run)" on grids whose rows can't satisfy them (Top Procedures, Query Store), so those verbs now **hide** rather than disable (per-row visibility binding) and each grid shows only the verbs that work; (2) the viewer never enabled **right-click-selects-row** (Lite and the Dashboard do), so a right-click could act on a *different* row than the one highlighted â€” its plan handlers read `CurrentItem` â€” now wired app-wide in `App.OnStartup`; (3) the **Query Store Regressions** grid had no plan options at all (its rows aggregate across plans, so there is no single stored plan to view) â€” it now offers **"Get Actual Plan (re-run)"** by QueryId via the same service path the Query Store history window uses, and the Query Store grid gains it too; (4) the live **Current Active Queries** grid lacked the right-click plan menu its stored sibling has â€” it now uses the snapshot row style, so Estimated / Live plan access is available by right-click. Stale docs corrected (the Regressions "no plan surface" note and the history windows' "Get Actual Plan is dropped"). Darling only; capability-pin grid-style + menu-command baselines updated.
- **Darling viewer: the first click on an unsorted grid column now sorts descending, and header-copy plus middle-click panning work on every grid** ([#1541]) â€” the viewer's data grids use an explicit `DarkGrid` style that carried no `BasedOn`, so it fully *replaced* the implicit `TargetType="DataGrid"` style instead of extending it, silently dropping the three attached behaviors that style contributes: `DataGridSortBehavior.SortDescendingFirst` (performance triage wants the largest CPU / duration / reads on top, so the first header click should sort descending rather than WPF's default ascending), `DataGridClipboardBehavior.FixHeaderCopy`, and `ScrollPanBehavior.EnableMiddleClickPanning`. Every query-tab grid (Top Queries, Top Procedures, Query Store, Regressions, snapshots) plus the FinOps and main-window grids therefore sorted ascending-first on the first click and lacked the copy / pan behaviors; Lite was unaffected because its grids use the implicit style. The three behaviors are now re-declared on all four `DarkGrid` definitions â€” a targeted setter add rather than `BasedOn`, which would also inherit the implicit style's vertical gridlines / clipping / padding and change the grids' appearance. Darling only.
- **Charts: finish the window-pin campaign so trend charts stay pinned to the selected window** ([#1540]) â€” Collection Health > Duration Trends still AutoScaled on load in both apps (the one chart #1483/#1484/#1487 missed), and every chart's Revert menu item + double-click AutoScaled to the data range (re-introducing the ~10% side dead-space on any interaction) instead of restoring the window pin. Both now pin X to the settable window (+ auto-Y); the Query Heatmap's categorical axis is unaffected.
- **Lite and Darling: Azure SQL DB deadlock and blocked-process capture now covers EVERY monitored database, not just the connection's own** ([#1346], [#1535]) â€” reported by a user whose nightly regression tests deadlock across ~50 Azure SQL databases while the Deadlocks page stayed empty even after the [#1251] fix unblocked the ring-buffer read. On Azure SQL DB these captures are *database-scoped* Extended Events sessions, and both the session-create and the ring-buffer read ran only on the connection's `Initial Catalog` â€” so a deadlock in any *other* database of the logical server was invisible by construction, while Azure's own server-wide `dl` telemetry (the user's proof query) showed them plainly. Meanwhile query stats, file I/O, index/object stats and database sizes already looped every database, so the app's own behavior taught users to expect whole-server coverage. **Both XE collectors now declare `RunsPerDatabase` on Azure**: the session is ensured and its ring buffer read once per monitored database (master skipped â€” logical master can't host database-scoped sessions; `ExcludedDatabases` honored via the shared list), exactly like the other per-database collectors. **Each database gets its own watermark** (new `PerDatabaseWatermarkColumn` contract on the shared definitions, honored by both hosts' runners): the sessions dispatch independently, so under the old single server-wide watermark one busy database's newer event would silence another database's older event still sitting in its ring buffer. `deadlocks` gains a trailing `database_name` column as that key (DuckDB v46 / Postgres V27 append + pinned-view refresh; `blocked_process_reports` has carried it since introduction) â€” on the per-database path both collectors stamp it with the capture database itself (a database-scoped session only captures its own database, and a row whose event XML carries no `currentdbname` could otherwise never advance its database's watermark, re-inserting every cycle until it aged out of the ring buffer); server-scoped platforms keep the victim/blocked process's `currentdbname` from the existing graph parse. **The [#1251] benign path also grew a read-back check**: a benign "already exists" (25631) proves the session exists, but NOT that this principal can see it â€” the reader joins `sys.dm_xe_database_sessions`, and a session invisible there (created by another principal, or present-but-stopped) reads zero rows forever while the collector records SUCCESS, the exact silent-empty shape of the report. After a benign error the session is now probed in the reader's own DMV and an invisible one is dropped and recreated under this principal; failure isolation is per database (one broken database never blocks the rest), and only all-databases-failed surfaces the collector unhealthy ([#1086] semantics). Darling's on-prem ensure additionally stops letting a benign deadlock-session collision skip the blocked-process ensure (each capture now ensures under its own benign filter), and the per-database "session verified" log lines drop to Debug so 50 databases don't write 100 Info lines per cycle. Grids need no changes â€” the per-process deadlock rows already display each process's database via the shared graph parser. Lite + Darling
- **Lite and Darling: an Azure SQL DB outage no longer permanently wedges database-scoped collection until the app is restarted** ([#1506]) â€” reported by a user monitoring Azure SQL DB from a machine whose public IP rotates daily: each time it changed, the server's firewall rule stopped matching and connections were rejected; re-adding the rule brought the server back, but Lite kept collecting nothing until it was restarted. **The cause was an error-classification bug, not a connection bug.** `IsMasterAccessDeniedError` decided whether a login is permanently unable to read `master` â€” the [#857] case, where a contained user exists only in a user database and database-scoped collectors must fall back to the connection's own catalog â€” but its error list also contained two errors that describe *reachability*, not *rights*: **40615** ("client with IP address â€¦ is not allowed to access the server", i.e. the firewall rejection this user hit every day) and **40613** ("not currently available, please retry later"). Worse, the resulting verdict was cached in a per-server dictionary that was only ever written `true`, so a single unlucky moment during an outage permanently demoted a perfectly healthy server. The codebase openly contradicted itself: 40613 sat in `RetryHelper.TransientErrorNumbers` as *retryable* while simultaneously being treated as *permanently fatal*. **Three fixes, applied to both SKUs.** (1) Both reachability errors are **removed** from the master-access list â€” a firewall rejection is not evidence about `master`, and falling back to a user database is futile anyway since the same rule blocks that connection too; they now propagate as ordinary collector errors: logged, visible in `collection_log`, retried next cycle. 4060 and 18456 stay, because a contained user genuinely does get them when opening `master`. (2) The verdict becomes a **throttle, not a life sentence** â€” it records *when* it was formed, expires after 15 minutes, and is **discarded outright the moment a server returns from an outage** (Lite tracks the offlineâ†’online edge in its scheduler loop; Darling hangs it off the existing `TryConnectAsync` connection edge that already fires "Server Restored"), because a judgement formed while a server was failing is untrustworthy by construction. A login that truly cannot see `master` still only re-probes 4x/hour, and the throttle is skipped entirely when there is no fallback database to throttle *to* â€” honouring it there would guarantee 15 minutes of failure without ever attempting to recover. (3) The failure is no longer **silent**: when master can't be enumerated *and* the connection has no target database to fall back on (`InitialCatalog` defaults to `master`, and a single-DB list of `master` is empty), every database-scoped collector iterated an empty list, wrote zero rows, and recorded **SUCCESS** â€” so the status bar cheerfully read "Collection: Running / Collectors: N OK" while nothing whatsoever was being collected. That case now throws, landing a real error in `collection_log` that names the fix (set a Database for the server); a test pins that the message can't be mistaken for the MFA-cancellation filter that would downgrade it back to a silent SKIPPED. **The root enabler was duplication:** Lite and Darling each carried their own copy of these error lists, which is precisely how the bug was ported from one to the other, and the transient list lived in Lite where no Darling test could see it. Both lists now live in one shared, dependency-free `SqlErrorClassification` (plain `int`s, no SqlClient), so they cannot be edited in one SKU and not the other â€” and the **disjointness invariant is asserted from both test suites**: no error number may be simultaneously "retry this" and "give up forever", which is the bug class itself and would have caught this at authoring time. Master enumeration also gains a retry it never had (a blip now costs a slower cycle instead of an ERROR row â€” though note it cannot itself prevent a verdict, since retryable and verdict-forming errors are disjoint by construction). Lite + Darling
- **Dashboard and Lite: the server-list version display no longer risks a crash on a two-part version string** ([#1510]) â€” six spots across the two apps' server-list / landing / health views hand-rolled `Version.TryParse(v, out p) ? new Version(p.Major, p.Minor, p.Build).ToString() : v`, which carries a latent throw: `Version.TryParse("3.1")` succeeds with `Build == -1`, and `new Version(3, 1, -1)` throws `ArgumentOutOfRangeException`. They are consolidated into one shared `PerformanceMonitor.Ui.VersionText` (`Parse` clamps `Build` up from -1 and strips SemVer `+build`/`-prerelease` suffixes; `Normalize` gives the three-part display form) â€” the UI-layer twin of the installer's `ScriptProvider.TryParseVersionCore` hardening from [#1498], now pinned by regression tests in the CI-wired `Lite.Tests`. Behavior-preserving for every realistic input (a real version or a status string); only the two-part throw changes.
- **Clean install is Azure SQL Managed Instance-safe, and a failed drop no longer leaves the database bricked** ([#1498]) â€” the clean-install teardown ran `ALTER DATABASE ... SET SINGLE_USER WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE` before `DROP DATABASE` to evict other connections. `SINGLE_USER` is non-modifiable on Azure SQL Managed Instance (`SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition') = 8`), so the whole teardown batch died on that line there; it is now gated to box SQL Server (MI drops directly, relying on there being no other sessions, which an installer teardown normally guarantees). And because `SINGLE_USER` and `DROP` are two separate autocommitted statements, a `DROP` that failed (another session racing the single-user slot) or hit the client command-timeout/cancel *after* `SINGLE_USER` committed left the database online but stranded in `SINGLE_USER` â€” semi-bricked. The teardown now restores `MULTI_USER` best-effort on a fresh connection before re-raising the original error (a client-attention timeout is invisible to a server-side `TRY/CATCH`, so this has to be C#-side); the Dashboard's server-drop path warns if that restore itself fails. Applies to `InstallationService.CleanInstallAsync` and the Dashboard's "remove server" drop; the SQL constructs were verified against Microsoft Learn for both box SQL Server and Managed Instance.
- **CLI installer: a password beginning with `--` gets a clear message instead of a misleading "password required"** ([#1498]) â€” a value like `--h7x` passed as the SQL-auth password is indistinguishable from a flag, so the positional-argument filter dropped it and the run then reported *"Password is required"* â€” for a password that *was* supplied. The error now adds a targeted note (only when a `--`-token that is not a recognized flag actually appeared) explaining that such a password must go through `PM_SQL_PASSWORD`. The parsing is unchanged â€” deliberately, since accepting `--`-prefixed values positionally is what would make a mistyped flag become a password.
- **Dashboard: a failed upgrade migration no longer stamps the database as successfully upgraded** ([#1498]) â€” `AddServerDialog` logged *"Upgrade failures detected. Continuing with full installation to ensure consistency..."* when an `upgrades/*` script failed, ran the full install over the partially-upgraded database anyway, and then wrote an `installation_history` row using `InstallationResult.Success` â€” which is `FilesFailed == 0` for the **install files only** and excludes upgrade failures entirely. That row was permanent damage: version detection reads the most recent `installation_status = 'SUCCESS'` row, and upgrade discovery only offers hops newer than the detected version, so the failed migration was stamped SUCCESS at the target version, the server then reported as current with the `ALTER`s never applied, and the hop was **never offered again** â€” silent schema drift recoverable only by a clean install. `ExecuteAllUpgradesAsync` already documented the contract ("*the caller aborts the whole install when totalFailureCount > 0*") and the CLI installer honored it; only the Dashboard's single-server path did not. The dialog now aborts on upgrade failure without running the install or writing a history row â€” leaving no `SUCCESS` row is exactly what makes the hop get re-offered, and upgrade scripts are written with guarded `ALTER`s so a re-run after fixing the error resumes cleanly â€” and folds the upgrade counts into the history write, matching the CLI. Relatedly, `ScriptProvider.FilterUpgrades` now throws on a version that is present but has no parseable numeric core instead of silently returning "no upgrades needed" (an empty result must never be the answer to "I could not read the version you gave me" â€” that path is what let a status string like `"Unreachable"` skip every migration while reporting zero failures), and `ExecuteAllUpgradesAsync` reports that as a failure so callers abort through the existing contract rather than hitting an unhandled exception. The **CLI**'s abort was itself nested inside `if (upgradeCount > 0)`, so a discovery failure (which reports zero hops) slipped past it â€” the CLI printed *"No pending upgrades found."* and ran the whole install; the abort now sits outside that block. Two latent version-parsing bugs the strict parse would have detonated are closed at the same choke point: `Version.TryParse("3.1")` succeeds with `Build == -1`, which the `Version` ctor rejects (now clamped), and `GetAppVersion` strips a `+metadata` suffix but not `-prerelease` â€” so a `3.2.0-rc1` `<InformationalVersion>` would have hard-aborted every upgrade. `FilterUpgrades` now normalizes SemVer suffixes at the choke point rather than relying on the seven call sites that each hand-strip them, mirroring `SingleInstanceDecision.ParseProductVersion`. The two decisions that can corrupt the ledger â€” "is installing safe?" (`InstallGuard`) and "are a repair's file failures the expected kind?" (`RepairOutcome`) â€” are extracted into `Installer.Core` and shared by both surfaces rather than hand-copied, and pinned by regression cases in `Installer.Tests`, which is CI-wired (`Dashboard.Tests` is not, so tests placed there could never fail a PR). One caveat worth recording: the ledger-lost probe is SQL, so its only test lives in `VersionDetectionTests`, which CI excludes because it needs a real SQL Server â€” that query was verified by hand against SQL Server 2022, but it cannot fail a PR. Found while reviewing [#963].
- **Dashboard: a failed version check no longer reinstalls over an existing database and reports it as up to date** ([#1498]) â€” `AddServerDialog`'s pre-install discovery called `GetInstalledVersionAsync` with the *soft*, null-returning overload. That method's own comment names the hazard: the installer passes `throwOnError: true` so "a transient/permission error can't be mistaken for 'database absent' and silently trigger a fresh install over an existing database (no upgrades, then logged as SUCCESS â€” the #538 hazard)", and only *soft callers* (the version column) keep the null fallback. But this call is not the version column â€” it is the discovery that decides install-vs-upgrade, the same class of caller as the CLI. So a connection timeout, a database left `OFFLINE`/`RESTORING`, or a permissions blip came back as `null`, read as "no database", and dropped the dialog into the fresh-install path: every migration skipped, install scripts run over the existing database, and `installation_history` stamped `SUCCESS` at the target version â€” stranding every pending hop exactly as above. Discovery now fails loudly and the dialog enters a new "status unknown" state that hides the install button and explains why; an `_installBlockedReason` guard also hard-blocks the install path, so the protection is structural rather than depending on a hidden button. The version is re-read â€” and the safety verdict re-run â€” against the connection the installer is about to *write* to, since the server box stays editable after a connection test.
- **A non-destructive repair path, so aborting on a failed migration doesn't leave you stuck** ([#1498]) â€” an upgrade script can fail *because* an object it `ALTER`s is missing or damaged, and before the abort above, the full install running afterwards would quietly recreate it. With the abort in place the only remaining escape hatch was Clean Install, which drops the database. **Dashboard** gains a "Repair: reinstall objects, skip upgrade scripts" option in Advanced Options (mutually exclusive with clean install; the upgrade-abort message now points at it), and the **CLI installer** gains `--repair` (mutually exclusive with `--reinstall`). Both run the idempotent install scripts *without* running migrations, so missing objects come back and the pending upgrade can then be re-run. Critically, a repair *that skips pending migrations* writes **no** `installation_history` row: it changes no version, and that table is the version ledger â€” echoing back a version we merely *read* is how a guess becomes a fact (`GetInstalledVersionAsync` returns the `0.0.0` sentinel as a fallback meaning "unknown, try every upgrade", and persisting that as a `SUCCESS` row would make the guess true). Writing nothing leaves the previous row as the version of record, so the pending upgrade is still offered. Note that a repair against a database with pending migrations is *expected* to report errors on a few procedures: the install scripts compile against the current schema (`install/23_process_blocked_process_xml.sql` reads a column the `3.0.0-to-3.1.0` migration adds) and `ALTER PROCEDURE` binds columns at compile time, so those objects cannot compile until the upgrade runs. A failed `CREATE OR ALTER` leaves the old body intact, nothing is damaged, and the upgrade recompiles them â€” so the "run the upgrade next" handoff is deliberately not gated on a clean repair.
- **`--repair` exit-code contract** ([#1498]) â€” a repair with a *pending upgrade* exits **0** even with failed files, because those failures are the expected compile errors described above; reporting them as a failure sent operators reaching for `--reinstall`, which drops the database. A repair with **no** pending upgrade has no such excuse, so a failure there still exits non-zero. A third case: when the server's recorded version cannot be read at all, the failures cannot be excused (exit non-zero) but an upgrade *is* still offered â€” those are two different questions, and answering both with one flag told operators "already at the current version, so there is no upgrade to apply" for a server with every hop pending. `--repair` also now refuses when there is no installation to repair, rather than silently performing a full fresh install and stamping the target version on a mistyped server.
- **Dashboard: retyping the server name no longer leaves the previous server's answers on screen** ([#1498]) â€” the Add/Edit Server box stays editable while the connection status, installed version, safety verdict and the Advanced Options checkboxes all describe whichever server was last *checked*. Nothing re-read the box, so every one of those could end up describing a different server than the one named on screen, and one of them is destructive: a ticked "clean install" (which runs `DROP DATABASE PerformanceMonitor` and drops the Agent jobs and Extended Events sessions) survived a retarget and could fire against a server that was never consented to, with no confirmation. Each verdict is now stamped with the server it was actually read from, and the dialog refuses to render one whose stamp no longer matches the box â€” a single structural check, rather than a re-check remembered at each of the dozen places that display or act on it. Related, from the same root: a connection test still in flight when an install started could wake up afterwards and republish its *pre-install* verdict over the finished result (restoring "Upgrade Now" on a server that had just been upgraded); **Save** could close the dialog the instant an install landed, which under SQL authentication skipped the monitoring-credentials prompt entirely and persisted the *installer's* account â€” routinely `sa` â€” as the ongoing monitoring login; and **Save** would skip the connection test altogether after an install, so a name typed into the box afterwards was saved without ever being contacted.
- **Cancelling an install is reported as a cancellation, not a failure â€” at every stage** ([#1498]) â€” a cancelled `SqlCommand` faults with `SqlException`, not `OperationCanceledException`, so two windows in the installer were counting a cancel as an ordinary failure and returning a normal result instead of surfacing the cancel: the clean-install teardown (before any install file runs), and the install file loop itself (a cancel during a *critical* file, which aborts the loop, or during the *last* file, which ends it â€” neither returns to the loop-top cancellation check). Both now convert a cancel into `OperationCanceledException` so the caller takes its cancellation path rather than announcing *"Installation completed with N error(s)."*
- **Cancelling a clean install no longer reports success over a database it just dropped** ([#1498]) â€” a clean install drops the three SQL Agent jobs, both Extended Events sessions, and then the database itself (`SET SINGLE_USER WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE`, `DROP DATABASE`) *before* the first install script runs. That was the single most destructive window in the installer, and it was the only one with no cancellation check in front of it â€” the first `ThrowIfCancellationRequested` sat *after* it. Worse, a cancelled `SqlCommand` faults with `SqlException`, not `OperationCanceledException`, so the clean-install error handler swallowed the cancel as an ordinary failure and returned **normally**: the caller's cancellation path never ran. Clicking **Cancel Install** mid-drop produced *"Installation completed with 1 error(s)"* over a database that was already gone, restored the version verdict the dialog had deliberately discarded for safety, and â€” because **Save** skips the connection test once that verdict is stamped â€” let the server be saved without ever being reconnected to. A cancel is now surfaced as a cancel, and a clean install that *fails* is treated the same way: no history row, no verdict, the install log left on screen, and installing blocked until the server is re-checked. Pinned by regression tests, which is possible because the fix lives in `Installer.Core` rather than in the dialog.
- **Dashboard: "Save" is no longer enabled on a server with no PerformanceMonitor database** ([#1498]) â€” the `Connected_NoDatabase` state disables Save deliberately: the intended paths are **Install Now**, or the explicit *"Skip, just add server"* link (which re-enables Save itself). A button-gating change made while fixing an unrelated overlapping-probe bug re-enabled Save unconditionally after every status check, which handed that consent away on the most ordinary path in the product â€” point at a bare instance, click Check for Updates, read *"No PerformanceMonitor database found"*, and Save is live. It is also the default button, so **Enter** would commit a server the Dashboard can read nothing from.
- **Dashboard: cancelling an edit no longer silently renames the server you were editing** ([#1498]) â€” the Add/Edit Server dialog writes its fields straight into the `ServerConnection` object it was handed, and in edit mode that object *is* the one `ServerManager` holds â€” not a copy. Clicking **Save** and then closing the dialog (ESC, Cancel, the X) while the connection test was still running let the test finish and complete the save anyway: the dialog was gone, `ShowDialog` had already returned "cancelled", and the caller never persisted anything â€” but the live object had already been mutated in place, and it reached disk on its own the next time any server's `last_connected` was written. A `PROD01` retargeted to `TEST99` and abandoned would come back as `TEST99` under `PROD01`'s id and credentials, and `PROD01` would stop being monitored, with nothing on screen ever having said so.
- **Dashboard: a failed or cancelled clean install no longer hides the install log** ([#1498]) â€” the clean-install path deliberately discards the pre-install version (the database is about to be dropped, so a verdict describing it would be a lie), and the "no verdict to restore" branch that handles that returned *before* the line that re-shows the installation panel â€” which the surrounding code had collapsed. The install log is the only place the actual SQL error text appears, and `DROP DATABASE` runs *before* the install scripts, so a cancel or failure at any point afterwards left the user with a dropped database, a single exception message, and no log. That branch also told them "this server's installed version was not read", which on that path is false â€” it was read, and deliberately discarded.
- **Both installers now refuse to run over a database that is NEWER than the binary** ([#1498]) â€” an older Dashboard or CLI installing over a server someone upgraded with a newer build would run its own older scripts, reverting every `CREATE OR ALTER` procedure and view to the older definitions, and then record the **lower** version as `SUCCESS`. Because upgrade discovery only offers hops *newer* than the current version, that case yields zero hops and zero failures, so no abort caught it. Both surfaces now hard-block such a server, as they do a version that will not parse at all. In the Dashboard everything is blocked, clean install included, so the block message carries the whole escape route: update the Dashboard, or use the CLI installer's destructive `--reinstall` (which drops the database first, so there is nothing left to downgrade).
- **Lite and Darling: the collector gate surface is collapsed to ONE shared layer, so Darling stops attempting collectors it can't run (no-msdb / Azure SQL DB / pre-2016) every cycle** ([#1500]) â€” the direct sequel to the [#1492] RDS fix, closing the whole gating-drift class it exposed. Lite gated collectors in TWO places â€” the shared `ICollectorSchemaInfo.AppliesTo` (which Darling also honors) AND its own host-side `IsCollectorSupported` â€” while Darling gated ONLY on `AppliesTo`, so every gate Lite kept solely in the second layer was silently ignored by Darling: on a monitored login without msdb access Darling attempted `running_jobs`/`job_history`/`agent_status` every cycle (each failing 229/916 and polluting collection-health), on Azure SQL DB it attempted `server_config`/`trace_flags`, and neither SKU version-gated `query_stats`/`query_store` off a pre-2016 box in the shared layer. **The second layer is gone.** `CollectorTargetInfo` gains a `HasMsdbAccess` flag (default true = assume access, matching the probe's NULL semantics), populated in BOTH hosts from the `HAS_DBACCESS(N'msdb')` value each already probed â€” Darling had it on `ServerRuntime` but never wired it into the gate (the exact missing link), Lite reads it from `ServerConnectionStatus`. Each gate moved into the correct collector's shared `AppliesTo`, matching Lite's exact prior condition: `ServerConfigCollector`/`TraceFlagsCollector` â†’ `!IsAzureSqlDb`; `QueryStatsCollector`/`QueryStoreCollector` â†’ `SqlMajorVersion == 0 || >= 13 || IsAzureSqlDb || IsAzureManagedInstance`; `RunningJobsCollector`/`AgentStatusCollector` add `&& HasMsdbAccess` atop their existing `!IsAzureSqlDb && !IsAwsRds`, and `JobHistoryCollector` adds `&& HasMsdbAccess` atop `!IsAzureSqlDb` (still NOT RDS-gated â€” it never touches `syssessions`). `AppliesTo` is hoisted from `ICollectorDefinition<TRow>` up to the non-generic `ICollectorSchemaInfo` so `CollectorCatalog.AppliesTo(name, target)` can evaluate the gate by name without the row type â€” the ONE surface both SKUs consult. Lite's `IsCollectorSupported` is DELETED; `RunCollectorAsync` now calls `CollectorCatalog.AppliesTo` pre-dispatch, preserving its clean SKIPPED log (a genuine skip with no `collection_log` row, vs. the SUCCESS/0-rows a gated collector would otherwise record) â€” behavior-identical for Lite (same gates, relocated), and INTENTIONALLY corrective for Darling (it now skips these targets). The gate CONDITION lives ONLY in each definition's `AppliesTo`, so the two hosts can't drift on it again. Covered by extended per-collector `AppliesTo` tests (the msdb / config / version dimensions) plus a new `CollectorGateSurfacePinTests` that pins every moved gate's truth table (Azure SQL DB / AWS RDS / no-msdb / pre-2016) AND asserts `CollectorCatalog.AppliesTo(name, target)` agrees with each definition's own `AppliesTo` for every catalog collector across every target â€” the proof both SKUs now gate identically off one surface. Lite + Darling
- **Darling viewer: the collector-schedule presets now include `job_history`, `agent_status`, and `default_trace_events`, matching Lite** ([#1495]) â€” the viewer's Aggressive / Balanced / Low-Impact preset tables (`CollectorSchedulePresets.Presets`, duplicated byte-for-byte from Lite's `ScheduleManager.s_presets`) were missing these three collectors in **all three** presets, so applying a preset in the Darling schedule editor left them pinned at their code-default cadence instead of the preset's â€” a drift flagged during the [#1494] review. Each preset now carries the three at Lite's exact interval (Aggressive 2 min, Balanced 5 min, Low-Impact 15 min); retention is untouched (it lives in the shared `CollectorScheduleDefaults`, which already carried all three, so `BuildDefaultSchedule` was already correct â€” only the preset-apply path drifted). The class docstring promised "a Darling.Tests pin asserts they cannot drift from Lite's intervals," but no membership pin actually existed â€” the existing `Presets_OnlyReferenceKnownCollectors` only checks the reverse direction (no *unknown* collectors), which is why the missing three went uncaught. A new `Presets_CoverTheSamePinnedCollectorSet` test mirrors Lite's own `SchedulePresets_CoverTheSamePinnedCollectorSet`, pinning the full 27-collector membership of every preset so a future collector added to one SKU but not mirrored into all three Darling presets fails here instead of shipping silently. Darling
- **Lite: the `v_job_history` and `v_default_trace_events` archive views no longer double-count rows after a 512 MB emergency reset** ([#1492]) â€” both views `UNION` the hot table with the parquet archive, but `ArchiveService.ArchiveAllAndResetAsync` archives all hot data to parquet AND wipes `collection_log`, so the next cycle re-collects recent history into the hot store while parquet still holds it. The local surrogate prefix id is a per-process counter (`CollectionIdGenerator`), so the re-collected rows get brand-new ids and the plain `UNION ALL` showed each logical event twice. `CreateArchiveViewsAsync` now dedups each on its SQL-Server-side natural key via `QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY <key> ORDER BY collection_time DESC) = 1`, keeping the newest-collected (hot) copy â€” `job_history` on `(server_id, instance_id)` (the `sysjobhistory` identity that survives `sp_purge_jobhistory`), `default_trace_events` on `(server_id, event_time, event_sequence)` (the trace `EventSequence`, paired with `event_time` so it stays distinct across the server restarts that reset it). Other archivable tables are unaffected â€” normal archival keeps hot and parquet disjoint, so only these two re-collect over still-archived rows. Covered by a new `ArchiveViewDedupTests` that stages the archiveâ†’resetâ†’re-collect sequence and asserts each natural key appears exactly once with the hot copy winning. Lite
- **Lite and Darling: `agent_status` and `running_jobs` are now gated off AWS RDS in BOTH apps** ([#1492]) â€” Lite skipped these on RDS host-side (`IsCollectorSupported`), but the shared collector `AppliesTo` only knew about Azure SQL DB, so Darling â€” which gates purely on `AppliesTo` â€” ran them on RDS and logged a collection error every cycle. `sys.dm_server_services` (agent_status) reads OS/service-control state a managed RDS instance does not expose, and `running_jobs` joins `msdb.dbo.syssessions`, which RDS blocks with "SELECT permission was denied" even for the master login (the table requires sysadmin, which RDS never grants). `CollectorTargetInfo` gains an `IsAwsRds` flag (populated in both hosts from the existing `DB_ID('rdsadmin')` probe), and `AgentStatusCollector`/`RunningJobsCollector` `AppliesTo` now check `!IsAwsRds` so both hosts skip identically â€” mirroring how the Azure gate already lives in both `AppliesTo` and Lite's `IsCollectorSupported`. Also corrects a stale `RunningJobsCollector` doc comment that claimed RDS collects, and updates the two `AppliesTo` tests. `job_history` still collects on RDS (it reads only `sysjobhistory`/`sysjobs`/`syscategories`, which RDS exposes). Lite + Darling
- **Dashboard: the cross-app `ThemeParityTests` pass again â€” the Dashboard theme palettes are re-synced to Lite** ([#1492]) â€” a Lite-side CoolBreeze palette change ([#1441]) plus accumulated `Slicer*`/foreground drift left shared color keys differing across all three themes (CoolBreeze/Dark/Light), failing the parity guard. Every differing shared key in `Dashboard/Themes/{CoolBreeze,Dark,Light}Theme.xaml` â€” the `ForegroundMuted`/`ForegroundDim` colors and the `Slicer*`/`PlanPanelMuted` brushes â€” is realigned to Lite's value (Lite is the source of truth), mostly by mirroring Lite's `{StaticResource ...}` references so the palettes stay coupled rather than re-drifting on the next base-color tweak; this also fixes a couple of Dashboard-internal literals that didn't match the theme's own accent. Dashboard-only (a deprecated app, but a legitimate parity bug). Dashboard
- **Lite, Dashboard, and Darling: anomaly-engine remainder Stage A â€” baseline-quality gate, one-fact wait-profile detection, and honest no-baseline rendering** ([#1491]) â€” builds ON the SAFE #1486 floors/sigma-cap.
